Careers at Tasklet
Build the AI workforce.
AI is moving from answering questions to owning work. Join a small team in San Francisco building AI employees that companies can trust with real responsibilities, around the clock.
All positions are full-time and in-person in our San Francisco office.

To apply, email our CEO Andrew (andrew@tasklet.ai) with a video introduction of yourself. Include your resume & any relevant links so we can learn more about you.
Your video must be 5 minutes or less and include the following:
- Who you are & why you want to join Tasklet

About Tasklet
Better models are only the beginning.
We believe every company will have AI employees. Not another chatbot to prompt or a workflow to babysit, but teammates with real responsibilities: closing the books, keeping a sales pipeline moving, helping customers, and following through on the work between messages. Tasklet is building the product that makes those employees possible.
Our vision is a team of virtual teammates that learn your business, collaborate with your people, and follow through around the clock. Customers already build custom agents, connect their business tools, and work with them in Tasklet, Slack, email, and text. We’re making it easier for an entire company to work with those agents—and for the people responsible to understand and control what they do.
The hard part is everything between a capable model and a colleague you can depend on. We combine models from multiple labs, swarms of parallel workers, cloud computers, and connections to business systems in one agent harness. Then we make that power understandable: what an agent owns, what it did, when it needs help, and what it is allowed to do next.
The AI labs are racing to build better models. We’re racing to turn that intelligence into a workforce businesses can actually rely on. We can use the best technology from any lab and focus our entire company on that problem. This is a chance to help define a new category of software—not add one more feature to the old one.
A small team with a proven track record.
Our team of 14 is based in San Francisco and is led by founders Andrew Lee (formerly founded Firebase & sold it to Google) and Jonny Dimond and includes Will Chambers, Hiranya Jayathilaka, Michael Lehenbauer, Michael Latman, Parker Chun, Christiana Stubblefield, Tyler Rockwood, Mark Robinson, Geoff Hamilton, Austin Haag, Brennan Murphy, and Dan Sundberg.
We’ve built the kinds of systems this moment demands: agent systems, cloud infrastructure, and productivity tools. That experience comes from building Shortwave, Firebase, and critical infrastructure at Google, Amazon, OpenAI, Salesforce, and elsewhere. Here, you’ll work directly with the people who built them—and own a meaningful piece of what comes next.
We run the company on our own AI employees. They handle real work in recruiting, support, and operations, and engineers direct parallel agents every day. If work can be delegated to an agent, we delegate it.
Backed with $42M from world-class investors.
We’ve raised over $42M from Union Square Ventures, Lightspeed Venture Partners, Y Combinator, Jeff Dean, Patrick & John Collison and others. Our most recent funding round was raised with a valuation cap of $500M.
Culture
A high-performance, results-focused culture.
Tasklet is a fast-paced, engineering-driven, results-oriented company. We aim to build a truly world-class team & an amazing work environment. These are the values we aspire to:
- Understand our users & solve their A+ problems
- You focus on the problems that really matter — not the ones you already know how to solve. You take responsibility for delivering experiences that our users love — end-to-end. This requires a deep understanding of our users, their workflows, and how AI agents can transform them.
- Relentlessly pursue a vision for excellence, but ship today
- In the world of AI agents, things change rapidly, and we intend to set the pace. You iterate with extreme urgency, shipping improvements daily that compound into transformative capabilities over time. You aim high, know where you're going, and don't stop until it's amazing.
- Lead
- You accomplish far more through teamwork than you could individually. You enable teamwork by being a leader, and you lead by doing, not just by talking.
- Take initiative
- You act like an owner and make great things happen without being asked. You try to be the change you want to see in the world. You believe nothing is someone else's problem.
- Be honest & truth seeking
- You’re committed to finding & communicating the truth. You follow evidence, not your ego. You speak your mind and let others speak theirs.
- Deliver business results
- You know that results — not effort — are what counts, and you take responsibility for our hardest & most important work: figuring out what needs to be done to get the right results. You own the results you produce, including your failures.
$20,000 referral bonus

Some eligibility restrictions apply:

A tale of two Nvidias
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
21:07:42 UTC
06:07:42 KST

We got two very different pictures of Nvidia this week, depending on whether you were focused on the company’s latest earnings report or the tech executives loudly calling for former CEO Jensen Huang’s return.
On the latest episode of TechCrunch’s Equity podcast, Kirsten Korosec, Tim De Chant, and I discussed the latest news from the AI chipmaker, which reported soaring revenue and insisted that it has no plans for Huang to return.
Kirsten described this as “the tale of two Nvidias this week,” with the company’s current executives assuring investors, “Hey, we’ve got this.” At the same time, “You’ve got Elon Musk and others out there really pushing to bring Jensen back, circulating a petition, Jensen himself saying that he’s prepared to come back.” (The company’s falling stock price certainly suggests that Wall Street isn’t feeling reassured.)
Keep reading for a preview of our conversation, edited for length and clarity.
Kirsten Korosec: Nvidia will no longer sell its H200 chips in China. This was coming directly from the interim CEO Colette Kress, and that coincides with the Trump administration also talking about closing off what we have discussed in the past, which is this idea of remote access — really closing and tightening all these restrictions [so] the chips can’t make it into China.
What was happening is that data centers were being set up right outside, in countries like Malaysia and elsewhere, and then companies within China were remote accessing these chips, and so that’s now also been shut down.
A lot is happening on the China front that is so much bigger than Nvidia, but Nvidia came out publicly and said, “Nope, we’re shutting this part down.” I’ll be very curious to see if this changes, maybe in the first half of 2027.
Tim De Chant: I’d love to be a fly on the wall in some of these Trump administration conversations, because they were much more stringent in terms of export controls at the end of the first Trump administration, and that continued through the Biden administration, where they really restricted Nvidia’s ability to export chips to China. I think that something changed in the last couple of years that really opened Nvidia’s ability to sell in the country.
Anthony Ha: One of the things that really surprised me as we were preparing for this episode was [learning that critics] of Nvidia’s stance towards China estimate that 20% of their business comes from China.
And again, that’s not something you’re going to see directly in their financials, because that’s not direct sales to Chinese companies. But they’re saying, “A lot of the chips that are getting sold to companies in Malaysia or other companies in East Asia or Southeast Asia are going to end up [used by companies in] China eventually.” And so that could be — if Nvidia is really serious about this, I think this is something that’s very much in flux — that could actually be a pretty significant blow to their bottom line.
Kirsten: Although Nvidia’s bottom line is doing just fine. In every possible way, their guidance was incredible. They beat expectations. They have a long pipeline.
It was really interesting because you have that happening, and then you have all this noise happening in social media, and also a number of other tech conferences were happening this past week — Dealbook Summit, there [were] a few others in which you had the highest profile people that you can think of in the tech world commenting on Jensen [Huang], and Jensen himself commenting.
So it’s like the tale of two Nvidias this week. You’ve got the earnings and the current executives really talking about, “Hey, we’ve got this.” In fact, that was the last line that Collette Kress said [on the earnings call]. And then at the exact same time, or in parallel, you’ve got Elon Musk and others out there really pushing to bring Jensen back, circulating a petition, Jensen himself saying that he’s prepared to come back. So it’s really quite the drama that we are definitely not done with, by the way.
Anthony: Although I think that what the folks who’d like to see Jensen Huang come back would say is that this most recent quarter is the last quarter under him. This is the [last of the] good times that we’re seeing under him. And so we don’t know how well Nvidia will do without him.
It seems like Nvidia’s public stance right now [on Huang’s possible return] is, “No, that’s not happening.” But Nvidia’s public stance a week and a half before that was, “We stand behind Jensen. We have every confidence in his leadership.”
So depending on the various pressures on them, you can imagine that changing. I don’t know what it would take for that to happen. I don’t necessarily think it’s that likely, but I also don’t think that just because they’re saying it’s not happening now doesn’t mean it will never happen.
Kirsten: Sure, never say never.
Tim: Right, never say never. I do think that to some extent, their steadfastness and how quickly they shot down the petitions asking for Jensen to return suggests there’s maybe a little bit more going on behind the scenes than we know on the outside. So that’s probably something to watch in the coming weeks.
Kirsten: I think that the board is trying to be in control here, and they don’t want to be forced into a decision because someone like Elon Musk is saying that it needs to happen. And so, even if there is a day in which Jensen returns — which I think is pretty unlikely, especially because the investigation into how much and who knew what about chips being sold, allegedly, secretly to China. We don’t know the full picture of that yet. But even if they did have the full picture, they want to present control, and they definitely don’t want to appear like they have deference to some other tech billionaire out there.
Tim: I’m sure they’re looking at what happened at OpenAI and not wanting to repeat that.
Anthony: I imagine the dynamic’s a little bit different, since Nvidia is a public company. So, I’m not an expert on this, but I would guess it would be much harder for you Jensen Huang to come back and just toss out a bunch of board members.

Toyota, Nissan, and Honda to Standardize Parts to Lower Costs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

Toyota, Nissan, and Honda to Standardize Parts to Lower Costs (caranddriver.com) 3
			
		 	
				 Car and Driver reports:
Japan's three largest automakers have agreed to begin standardizing vehicle parts in an effort to reduce costs, according to Automotive News... 
In the short term, the standardization efforts will begin with unified quality criteria for steel, consistent protocols for plastics and resins, and a universal color-coding scheme for wiring harnesses. In the long term, the Japan Automobile Manufacturers Association has plans to move to more components that cover the entire car...  The push to simplify and lower costs comes as Japan's seven automakers, including Toyota, Nissan, Honda, Mazda, Subaru, Mitsubishi, and Suzuki, work to fend off the increasingly competitive list of Chinese automakers.  
Long-time Slashdot reader sinij asks a related question.  "Can we get a standard infotainment port/spec so we can have third-party market for this? Just like we used to have for stereo systems?"

The UN fails to agree on rules regulating ‘killer robots’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
20:03:37 UTC
05:03:37 KST

One of the items on the agenda for UN diplomats meeting in Geneva this week was working towards rules regarding the use of autonomous weapons, or so-called “killer robots.” Eventually, representatives left the meetings agreeing to “start discussing options” after a largely fruitless week of negotiations.
The UN fails to agree on rules regulating ‘killer robots’
Countries agree to ‘start discussing options’ for rules around autonomous weapons.
According to NPR:
Before the negotiations got underway, the International Committee of the Red Cross, the guardian of the Geneva Conventions, said it was urgent for legally binding rules to be drafted within a year. That call was backed by the U.N. secretary-general and by Pope Leo.
The compromise reached was that countries would instead have two years to propose regulations. Negotiations between countries on which regulations to adopt and how to enforce them will likely drag on well beyond that. Additionally, room was left for some of those yet-to-be-adopted regulations to remain “optional.”
Humanitarian organizations and some countries see a need to urgently adopt legally binding rules as semi-autonomous weapons and drones have been responsible for a significant number of civilian deaths in conflicts around the globe, including in Ukraine, Gaza, and Sudan.
According to NPR, Russia, Israel, and the US proved to be the primary obstructions:
On day one, Russia delayed proceedings by objecting to the meeting’s agenda. Then the United States proposed a five-year window to discuss nonbinding, optional guidelines for autonomous weapons.

Americans Enters Post-Penny World, Businesses Now Allowed to Round Cash Transactions to Nearest Nickel
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Americans Enters Post-Penny World, Businesses Now Allowed to Round Cash Transactions to Nearest Nickel (nbcnews.com) 36
			
		 	
				Friday U.S. President Trump "signed the Common Cents Act, which now lets businesses round cash transactions to the nearest nickel, into law," reports CBS News:
It sets standards for cash transactions that businesses and consumers can use when exact change isn't available, as the penny is phased out. The legislation calls for cash transactions to be rounded up or down to the nearest 5 cents...  Before its passage, businesses could be sued if they failed to give customers exact change, even when rounding up in a patron's favor.  
CBS includes this quote from an official NACS, an advocacy group for the convenience/fuel retailing industry.  "Retailers have been looking for federal guidance on processing cash transactions since penny production ended. 
NBC News points out that the U.S. Mint said it would still produce collector's versions of pennies in "limited quantities," reporting on what has and hasn't changed now:
The penny can still be used as legal tender "for all debts, public charges, taxes, and dues," according to the new law. The Treasury Department said last year that the Federal Reserve will continue to recirculate the approximately 114 billion pennies that are in existence "for as long as possible...."   And, as the nickel replaces the penny at the bottom of the U.S. Mint's currency list, that coin can now be manufactured with an inner layer of zinc and an outer layer of nickel [according  to the newly-signed law]. It is currently produced with nickel and copper, which costs more than zinc.

A Belated And HUGE Thank You!
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21

A Belated And HUGE Thank You!
Posted on November 21, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 4 Comments
I knew when I started my GoFundMe that I already had the support of some readers based on their comments, which was very reassuring going into this. However, the support I have gotten has truly stunned and amazed me, and I genuinely feel so, so grateful. I didn’t think I’d hit my goal, let alone in just a few hours.
I never thought that so many people would rally behind this particular cause, but then again I should’ve thought about how cat-loving this audience tends to be. Y’all’s love of cats and generosity is allowing me to do my best in caring for these cats. I wish I could tell them how much you are all rooting for them, and how much you have given to see them healthy, safe, and fed.
I was planning to share my GoFundMe on Instagram and Bluesky to get some traction, but after posting it on the blog it only took a few hours to knock my goal out of the park. I feel truly lucky to have such amazing and kind readers. Even if you didn’t donate, y’all’s encouraging comments and motivational support have been so helpful and uplifting. Every like on the photos I post on Bluesky warms my heart.
So whether you’re supporting from afar and keeping these kitties in your thoughts, or you’ve shared the GoFundMe link, or you donated even five bucks, thank you so very much.
One thing I did want to say is that I noticed the GoFundMe sent out automated messages to each person who donated, and in most cases it looked like I had written it, rather than it being an automated thank you. I didn’t know it would send out auto-thank-you’s and I certainly didn’t think it would try to sound like an actual person, namely me. This is actually upsetting to me because it feels very impersonal towards y’all and feels strange to me to see that “I” sent you a message when I didn’t write those words at all.
So, I’m going to go back through and send each person an actual thank you written by me. Please disregard the automated robotic sounding thank you. I prefer my words to be my own.
(Update: after going back to GoFundMe to do what I said what I was going to, it turns out I can’t message y’all unless you message me first! Which like four people did do and I did reply to them. So if you want a more personal thank you just say hey :))
Also, if anyone would like proof of receipts or just to be told what all I’ve been spending money on, feel free to email me! I’m committed to complete financial transparency in this mission.
Also also, I didn’t reply to comments on my previous post on time before they closed, so I’m going to go back and reply now. I value each of them and want to reply accordingly.
Just for a general update, Big Grey has been getting his antibiotic everyday and is eating with no problems at all. Kevin and Tiffany both have upper respiratory infections and it affected Tiffany’s eating because she was all stuffy and nauseous, so on top of each of their long acting antibiotic shots, she got an anti-nausea shot and an appetite stimulant. Plus, she was a bit dehydrated so she got some subcutaneous fluids. She immediately showed great interest in food after that and has been drinking water!
They both have ear and eye drops and a new oral antibiotic, as well, since their eyes keep being leaky and goopy and they’re sneezy. Aside from being lil’ sickies, Tiffany and Kevin are two BIG ol’ cuddlebugs. Actual lovebugs. They are two peas in the cuddlepod. You could power a city with their purrs!
In other news, Big Grey is getting fixed on Monday and I will take that opportunity to ask my vet about a dentist for him.
Let me know if you have any other questions, and have a great day! And seriously, thank you all so much. Cat photo!
-AMS

Will AI-Fueled Cyberattacks Lead to Better Funding for Critical Open Source Projects?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

Will AI-Fueled Cyberattacks Lead to Better Funding for Critical Open Source Projects? (politico.eu) 12
			
		 	
				Remember that famous XKCD comic joking that all modern digital infrastructure rests on "a project some random person in Nebraska has been thanklessly maintaining since 2003."
Politico Europe's cybersecurity reporter wonders if this recent spate of outages suggests that supporting block is coming loose?  (Especially after Politico reported Thursday that internal services at EU institutions went offline after a major AI-fueled cyberattack.)
Authorities blame the sudden wave of instability on the capacity of new AI tools to detect vulnerabilities in existing code and for bad actors to exploit them...    Interviews with more than a dozen cybersecurity leaders and those working on maintaining the internet show the scale of the challenge to keep the web up and running, in a world where the pace of finding vulnerabilities has rapidly and permanently accelerated. Since the web's early days, an unruly cast of idealist engineers and non-profit foundations have been essential for looking after decades-old critical code that's been used to build our digital world. This small army of largely volunteer coders now say they can't keep up with the power and pace of AI. "We're running on fumes," said Robin Bender Ginn, executive director of the OpenJS Foundation, which looks after JavaScript, among the most widely used programming languages. "It keeps me up at night."
Cybersecurity agencies around the world rang the alarm last month about major flaws detected in PHP, another critical coding language that underpins 70% of the web. This week, similar scares have spread over Java and NGINX, which also fuel large swathes of technology.   Helmut Reisinger, the European chief executive of cybersecurity giant Palo Alto Networks, said in an interview that the company is seeing "more or less" ten times as many incidents compared to about four months ago. He predicts the constant drumbeat of attacks and scares in the last month is going to become the "new norm...."   
The rise of AI models and their capabilities of finding software flaws is putting more pressure on open-source software engineers, who are inundated with reports of vulnerabilities — some of which are false or exaggerated.  Reports of flaws discovered by AI are "quite verbose," Ginn said.   Juhan Lepassaar, the head of EU cyber agency ENISA, said in an interview that the agency has observed a similar pattern of false positives. 
While some hope the AI-fueled attacks "will put the needs of open source software on the map and prompt an intervention," according to the article, "many of those steeped in the ecosystem... have their doubts."
"I'm a bit pessimistic," says Nils Adermann, a PHP Foundation board member.  "I worry that people will just not care enough to actually do anything about it."

Trump says reported FBI database hack is ‘not good’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
18:44:53 UTC
03:44:53 KST

The FBI is investigating an apparent hack of the National Crime Information Center (NCIC), according to reports in CNN and the Associated Press.
The NCIC is the online database used by federal and local law enforcement to share information about warrants, background checks, and more. The scope of the breach does not yet appear to be clear.
A former FBI official told CNN that they’re not just concerned about “whether hackers gained access to sensitive information, although that is an issue, but whether they could compromise the accuracy of the information that officers rely on.”
President Donald Trump addressed the reports during an interview Saturday with Fox News, saying, “We just heard about it and I will say that it’s not good.” At the same time, Trump also said that as a result of the breach, “maybe we’ll learn things that we would have never learned otherwise because, you know, you had the Russia, Russia, Russia hoax.”
In September, the FBI reportedly told agents and support staff that their personal information had been stolen in a cyberattack.

Upload2Shell · CVE-2026-125396
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
18:29:29 UTC
03:29:29 KST

Debian 13 CUPS
Ghostscript 10.05.1~dfsg-1+deb13u1
- Access
- Anonymous IPP Print-Job to a shared network queue
- Observed
- Command on the host as uid=7(lp)
- Proof
- One remote run; separate 20/20 local conversions
A use-after-free in Ghostscript’s Type 1 font loader lets a crafted document cross Ghostscript’s -dSAFER sandbox, alter native path controls, and execute attacker-chosen commands as the rendering process.
Ghostscript sits beneath print, email, upload, and preview workflows. Our ImageMagick research showed how files named .png or .jpg can still reach Ghostscript through image conversion. The index below records confirmed affected paths alongside additional applications whose renderer routes we traced.
Upload2Shell / public record
From the first escape to the downstream reports, fixes, and publication.
We found a Type 1 font-loader path out of Ghostscript's -dSAFER boundary. A crafted document gained native access inside the rendering process.
A signed-out request for an attacker-hosted file reached Ghostscript in Google's document renderer. We sent the production proof to Google.
Core's upload path exposed the renderer to Author+ users and any Contributor granted upload_files. Public upload fields in multiple plugins required no WordPress login. WordPress shipped its separate CVE-2026-65640 fix.
We filed the Type 1 loader flaw upstream as Artifex Bug 709684. Our broader audit also produced other independent Ghostscript findings.
Artifex released the fix for the Type 1 loader flaw.
We confirmed a production document-processing finding in our broader Ghostscript research. Its individual root and proof belong to a separate case study.
Ghostscript 10.08.0 entered Debian unstable. The Debian 13 security update followed later.
Confirmed unauthenticated remote command execution: an IPP job to a shared queue ran on the host as lp. We sent the network reproduction to Debian Security.
Confirmed remote execution after an external sender's attachment was thumbnailed when its email was viewed. The tested ImageMagick delegate ran the proof as www-data.
Confirmed command execution in the Roundcube thumbnail path shipped with Plesk Obsidian. We reported the result to WebPros.
Confirmed unauthenticated remote command execution through a public registration widget with an upload field enabled. We reported the route to the plugin team.
Confirmed unauthenticated remote command execution through a public PDF form configured to store files in the WordPress Media Library. We sent the report to the plugin team.
Confirmed remote command execution when a configured document inbox imported and rendered an external email attachment. We sent the worker-chain report.
Confirmed command execution through an open wiki's upload and resize path using its optional ImageMagick backend. We sent the report.
Confirmed command execution through PdfHandler on a wiki configured for anonymous PDF uploads. We sent the MediaWiki reproduction to Wikimedia Security.
Confirmed command execution in the preview worker through an authenticated upload or an upload-share capability.
Confirmed a production route in our broader Ghostscript research. The individual access path, root, and proof will appear with its case study.
Debian published its first stable-security Ghostscript update after our report. The current fixed security build followed in October.
Debian published 10.05.1~dfsg-1+deb13u3, the Debian 13 security build now marked fixed for Upload2Shell.
Roundcube restricted unsafe attachment image types before ImageMagick conversion under CVE-2026-114727.
USN-8949-1 included our Ghostscript font-loader finding and fixed package versions for seven Ubuntu releases, including Ubuntu Pro and Legacy branches.
We submitted assignment requests for the Type 1 loader flaw after the upstream fix and downstream reports.
The Ghostscript root received its public identifier. Individual case studies and PoCs will be linked from the product table as they appear.
Google fixed the affected image and document processing path before our public case study.
Upstream Ghostscript and tracked WordPress, Debian, and Ubuntu fixes are available. We will link the product-specific posts, proofs, and remaining vendor responses as each is published.
Another print filter, thumbnailer, or document preview may be the next route into the interpreter. New results will be added with their own trigger, evidence, and fix.
The times on 17–18 September mark when the vendor reports were sent. Technical evidence and access conditions are in the index and writeup. Dropbox and the redacted services belong to separate Ghostscript findings.
Evidence index Updated 18 November 2026
A single interpreter surfaced behind very different file-processing features. Each entry shows who can supply the file, where it goes, and what we observed or traced.

The first ten entries record Upload2Shell execution. Gradio records a live HTTP-to-Ghostscript handoff; the remaining entries map application code to image-processing sinks.
02 / 02
Image and document processing · Ghostscript 10.05.0
Patch status
Ghostscript 10.08.0 fixes Upload2Shell. Debian backported the upstream change into its security package. Ubuntu released fixed Ghostscript packages for seven supported releases on 21 October 2026; its notice described our font-loader finding before the CVE number was assigned. Application teams also closed routes into the renderer: WordPress fixed the Author+ upload path under CVE-2026-65640, and Roundcube restricted unsafe attachment image types before ImageMagick conversion under CVE-2026-114727. The technical writeup lists distribution package versions.
We found several other Ghostscript issues with lower or comparable impact and reported them upstream. Ghostscript sits beneath more file-processing features than most applications expose directly. We expect the security community to find additional software that reaches this vulnerability through uploads, thumbnails, print jobs, and document imports.

Inkling-2-Mini in 5.4 GB Memory
Unknown

18:02:19 UTC
03:02:19 KST

LookAtMyQuant is a quantization compiler. It measures how much each stored piece of a model hurts the output when compressed. It then picks a code for each piece so the whole model fits a size budget with the least damage.
On Gemma 4 E2B, its GGUF has 12× lower KL than Q4_K_M at the same file size. The method and every result are on the site.
The code is under the MIT license.
LookAtMyQuant 2 takes Qwen3.5-4B to 1.23 GB, 2.3 bits per weight. The file solves 330 of 540 exact-answer math problems. Prism ML's Bonsai 2 Ternary 4B solves 328 at 1.20 GB, and the original solves 359. Its KL from the original on held-out text is 0.45 nats, against 0.59 for Bonsai.
The recipe is post-training quantization with trellis codes, calibrated on the model's own worked math solutions with digit tokens weighted 10×, and with bits allocated per row by Fisher information. 4,000 steps of distillation follow, in which every layer is re-quantized once from the original weights. The page has the findings, the full recipe and its cost, about $34 of GPU time.
The recipe's code is here, and the next section runs it. The 1.23 GB file itself is the lm2-qwen35-4b-1.2gb release, packed for the Apple Silicon runtime.
The same recipe takes Qwen3.8-27B from 53.7 GB to 7.2 GB, 2.1 bits per weight, calibrated and distilled on English prose and chat, code and math. The file solves 401 of the 540 math problems, as many as the original, and Unsloth's UD-IQ2_XXS GGUF of the same size solves 348. It also scores above that GGUF on HumanEval (73.8% against 67.1%), MMLU (78.8% against 75.0%) and GSM8K (94.5% against 92.7%).
Beside it sits a 4-bit DSpine drafter (1.02 GB, converted from tencent/Qwen3.8-27B-DSpine-b16 with runtime_mlx/convert_dspine.py and packed with pack_drafter.py), which roughly doubles decode speed on math and code on Apple silicon.
The same compiler and proximal fine-tuning (SQYFR), extended to a 26.4B physical / 5.4B active 3×-looped MoE (L6–17 looped 3× = 48 logical visits, 128 experts top-8), compresses thinkingmachines/Inkling-2-Mini from 18.89 GB of text weights to 5.38 GB (1.63 bits/weight) and 8.87 GB (2.69 bits/weight) using 4 KB SRAM hashed block-vector trellis codes (ht2/ht3), row trellis codes (rtr1/rtr4), and a tr2 floor on the tail lm_head bucket (head.7).
Evaluated on the same BF16 teacher, openai/gsm8k (test[:200], greedy, reasoning_effort="none"), all 164 executable openai/openai_humaneval problems, 4 Vision VQA tests (with the untouched 637 MB BF16 vision tower), and 16+13-language TextMix windows:
At 5.38 GB, Inkling-2-Mini-LookAtMyQuant-5.4GB outscores Unsloth's 9.14 GB UD-Q2_K_XL on both GSM8K and HumanEval at 41% smaller size (up from 76/200 and 24/164 untuned). At 8.87 GB, Inkling-2-Mini-LookAtMyQuant-8.9GB matches the BF16 original on GSM8K and cuts KL 2.34× on English/chat/code and 29.6% across all 13 languages below UD-Q2_K_XL. Both run directly through lamq and runtime_mlx with custom Apple Silicon Metal kernels (including M5 MetalPerformancePrimitives MoE prefill + FlashAttention-2 SDPA + exact unbiased temp > 0 speculative sampling, plus opt-in dynamic sigmoid Top-K routing, tight-margin verify, and N-gram prompt lookup), optionally paired with the 178.9 MB 4-bit DSpark drafters: on M5 Max, 5.4 GB reaches 1,807 tok/s T=512 prefill (1,047 tok/s at T=128), 75.3 tok/s serial decode, and 124.4 tok/s median lossless drafted decode (142.9 tok/s math, 126.5 tok/s reasoning, 122.2 tok/s code, 104.0 tok/s prose; up to ~150–160 tok/s with opt-in top6 / max_logit_gap=0.30; 5.98 GB active RAM), and 8.9 GB decodes at 63.5 tok/s serial and 106.0 tok/s median (125.8 tok/s reasoning, 123.8 tok/s math; 9.41 GB active RAM).
On Apple silicon, install the runtime package directly (this installs mlx, mlx-lm, numpy, and huggingface-hub without the PyTorch compiler dependencies):
pip install git+https://github.com/ShinMegamiBoson/lookatmyquant.git
From a local clone, pip install -e . installs the lamq command, or run python -m runtime_mlx without installing.
Command line. Pass either a local model folder or a Hugging Face repo ID:
lamq generate shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB \
    -p "What is 17 times 23? Show the steps."
lamq generate shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB \
    --drafter shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB-drafter \
    -p "Write a Python function that merges overlapping intervals." --stats
lamq chat shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB \
    --drafter shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB-drafter
lamq generate shinmegamiboson/Inkling-2-Mini-LookAtMyQuant-5.4GB \
    --drafter shinmegamiboson/Inkling-2-Mini-LookAtMyQuant-drafter \
    -p "What is 17 times 23? Show the steps." --stats
lamq generate reads stdin when -p is omitted, supports --raw, --system, --thinking, --temp, --mode linear|chat-fork|copy-wide, --no-adaptive, and --json. lamq info <model> inspects a packed model's format and dimensions without loading weights.
Python integration. runtime_mlx.load returns an (model, tokenizer) pair compatible with mlx_lm.generate and mlx_lm.stream_generate (auto-detecting opgraph-packed-1 models such as Qwen3.5-4B / Qwen3.8-27B and lamq.json exports such as Gemma 4 E2B):
from mlx_lm import generate
from runtime_mlx import load
model, tok = load("shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB")
prompt = tok.apply_chat_template(
    [{"role": "user", "content": "What is 17 times 23? Show the steps."}],
    add_generation_prompt=True,
    enable_thinking=False,
)
print(generate(model, tok, prompt, max_tokens=200))
For streaming, multi-turn chat, or speculative decoding through one interface, use Runtime:
from runtime_mlx import Runtime
rt = Runtime.load(
    "shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB",
    drafter="shinmegamiboson/Qwen3.8-27B-LookAtMyQuant-7.2GB-drafter",
)
for chunk in rt.stream("Solve 3x + 4y = 31 and 5x - 2y = 17.", max_tokens=256):
    print(chunk.text, end="", flush=True)
Install the dependencies and download the model and WikiText-2. The code reads SmolLM2-360M's config when it starts, so do this first.
pip install -r requirements.txt
python -c "from huggingface_hub import snapshot_download as d; d('HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M')"
python -c "from datasets import load_dataset as l; l('Salesforce/wikitext', 'wikitext-2-raw-v1')"
Measure. This compresses one piece at a time, leaves the rest exact, and records the KL to the original model. The full set for this model, 2,312 measurements, took about 50 minutes on an L40S.
python compiler.py measure --model HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M --part linear --layers 0-3 --out results/batch/my_measurements.json
Allocate. This picks one code per piece, with the least summed KL inside the budget. It needs no GPU. This command uses the SmolLM2-360M measurements in this repository, with a budget of 3.044 bits per weight.
python compiler.py allocate --sens 'results/batch/ccSens*.json' --target disk --items linear --bits 3.044 --out results/compiler/plans/my_plan.json
The plan it writes has the same widths as results/compiler/plans/sm_lin_3044.json.
Build and score. This quantizes the model to the plan, one matrix at a time. It then reports perplexity and KL to the original on WikiText-2 test.
python quantize.py --program plan --model HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M --plan results/compiler/plans/my_plan.json --out results/my_run.json
Our run of that plan is in results/batch/cc_sm_lin_3044.json. It reached perplexity 16.64 and KL 0.0926, against an original perplexity of 15.31. Add --layers 1 --calib 8 --eval 2 for a quick check that everything works.
Each step is a named job; python run_batch.py --list prints its command. The jobs need Qwen3.5-4B in models/Qwen3.5-4B on the volume and the text mix (modal run --detach modal_fetch.py --text). Every file they make goes to lm2/ on the volume.
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs lm2Text
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs lm2Calibration,lm2Solutions
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs lm2RowImportance
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs lm2Build
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs lm2Finetune
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs lm2Math,lm2Math216,lm2TextKL
lm2BuildSmoke, lm2FinetuneSmoke and lm2MathSmoke run the same code on two layers, 60 steps and 36 problems, in a few minutes each.
The row plan. lm2Build reads results/compiler/rows/q35_rowplan_1p2GB.json, which gives 1 to 4 bits to each of 1,520 row groups. row_allocation.py made it. Its build-control command builds and packs a control with a fixed split of rows, prepare-rows allocates rows inside the control's packed bytes with rowgroup_alloc.py, and build-rows builds under the plan and checks the packed size against it.
The codebooks. The four e-means codebooks the build decodes with are in cache/codes/. --trellis-fit lloyd gives the earlier ones, and a plan made before the change keeps them unless the flag says otherwise.
Recent equal-byte recipe improvements. Two zero-byte additions are available in the compiler and fine-tuning scripts:
- Softmax-Fisher token table ranking (--embed-order sfisher-mix in compiler.py measure and quantize.py). Ranks tied embedding/output buckets by a 50/50 blend of output Softmax-Fisher E[p_v (1 - p_v)] and corpus frequency (and untied output heads by Softmax-Fisher alone).
- Mid-training equal-byte row reallocation (--realloc-at 1000 in finetune.py). Paired with --rebuild-at 1000, measures each layer's continuous-vs-stored proximal KL gap KL(all P) - KL(layer = W) to shift bits toward deep unabsorbed layers while keeping exact file size (--realloc-max-delta 1). Combined with sfisher-mix on Qwen3.5-4B at identical 1.23 GB (2.297 bpp), WikiText-2 KL fell from 0.425 to 0.387 (−9.0%) and 13-source KL from 0.447 to 0.378 (−15.5%, improving all 13 sources).
Where these jobs differ from our run. Our GSM8K solutions were written earlier, with thinking always off. Our text KL on 13 sources used 52 windows picked from the text mix by a separate tool; --sources mix takes the mix's own evaluation windows, on which our file scores 0.44 where the page reports 0.45.
run_batch.py names every job, and modal_app.py runs them. modal_fetch.py downloads models and the text mix used for calibration and evaluation.
modal run modal_app.py::setup
modal run --detach modal_fetch.py --text
modal run --detach modal_fetch.py --models google/gemma-4-E2B-it
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs ccMG4E2Noise,ccMG4E2Spot,ccMG4E2EmbKv
The runs behind the site used these machines, each with 8 CPU cores:
gguf_compiler.py makes plans out of llama.cpp's own tensor types, so the result runs in llama.cpp as it is. public_eval.py scores public quantizations the same way as our plans. Both run inside the image in modal_gguf.py, which builds llama.cpp with CUDA.
quantize.py --export <folder> writes a plan in the format runtime_mlx/ reads. A plan with trellis codes also needs --trellis-rows 16,8, which runs each trellis path along a row of its matrix, so that decoding an entry reads only that row. On Apple silicon, pip install . installs mlx and mlx-lm; requirements.txt holds the PyTorch compiler dependencies.
On Gemma 4 E2B at 2.5 GB, the trellis plan has 26% lower KL than the Lloyd–Max plan and decodes about 22% slower. runtime_mlx/test_trellis_gen.py and runtime_mlx/test_trellis.py check the trellis kernels against the matrices the quantizer built.
python runtime_mlx/bench.py lamq path/to/export
runtime_mlx/fast_q35.py decodes the packed 1.2 GB Qwen3.5-4B file one token at a time, straight from its trellis codes. runtime_mlx/drafter_q35.py adds speculative decoding. A small drafter proposes the seven tokens after the model's latest one in a single pass. The model then checks all eight positions in one pass over its coded weights (runtime_mlx/block_q35.py) and keeps the proposals that match its own choice, so the text is exactly its own greedy text.
On an M5 Max, over 30 held-out prompts of up to 256 tokens, generation with the drafter was 2.0 times as fast: 176 tokens a second against 88, timed in alternation in one process on 2026-11-11. A check kept 4.3 tokens on average. Over eight everyday prompts it was 1.5 times as fast. The gain follows how predictable the text is: 2.5 times on algebra, 2.0 on code, 1.2 on chat and 1.05 on a story, and one of the 30 held-out prompts ran slower with the drafter. The text matched plain greedy decoding on every prompt. These are speeds after the first token; reading the prompt takes about 0.1 s either way.
Two later changes are in this code. Each was measured against the version before it on 40 paired generations: proposing from the 96,033 most common tokens was 6.0% faster, and picking the winning token inside the packed product was 5.3% faster. Linear mode also enables self-calibrating adaptive fallback by default (adaptive=True; pass --no-adaptive to disable): when rolling acceptance drops below the measured break-even ratio of drafted-check latency to serial-step latency (accounting for cache flush and drafter context replay), it pauses drafting for short exact serial bursts and resumes once acceptance recovers, preserving bit-exact greedy parity while avoiding slowdowns on low-acceptance prose.
The drafter is a DSpine model, trained with SpecForge from the published DFlash drafter for Qwen3.5-4B on the 1.2 GB file's own greedy answers. modal_drafter.py runs the training as one Modal job, and it cost $42. On 300 held-out prompts the trained drafter gets 4.65 tokens per check, against 4.30 for the stock DFlash drafter.
pack_drafter.py stores the drafter at 4 bits a weight in the build's own code, with no calibration data: 464 MB against 1.83 GB at 16 bits. On 60 held-out prompts the 4-bit drafter kept 4.35 tokens per check against 4.38, and the text was the same. drafter_q35.py loads either form and writes the packed one out dense as it loads. The 4-bit drafter is the lm2-drafter-4bit release. It runs beside the packed 1.2 GB file, the lm2-qwen35-4b-1.2gb release.
gh release download lm2-qwen35-4b-1.2gb --repo ShinMegamiBoson/lookatmyquant -D q35-1.2gb
gh release download lm2-drafter-4bit --repo ShinMegamiBoson/lookatmyquant -D drafter-4bit
python runtime_mlx/bench_drafted.py q35-1.2gb drafter-4bit
The benchmark defaults to linear verification. Use --mode chat-fork to try a second candidate at an ambiguous early position, or --mode copy-wide for known contiguous copies. Copy mode uses the source cursor and tunes the gate/up and down projections for 16-slot checks. Both modes verify all emitted tokens with the packed target. To benchmark the chat fork, run:
python runtime_mlx/bench_drafted.py q35-1.2gb drafter-4bit --mode chat-fork --prompts chat
For a copy benchmark, pass --mode copy-wide --heldout path/to/copy-prompts.json. The JSON entries carry source, prompt_len, and input_ids from the packed model's tokenizer.
The checks, and the packer for a drafter of your own:
python runtime_mlx/test_fast_q35.py
LAMQ_Q35_PACKED=path/to/packed python runtime_mlx/test_block_q35.py
python runtime_mlx/check_drafter.py path/to/packed path/to/drafter
python runtime_mlx/bench_drafted.py path/to/packed path/to/drafter --heldout path/to/drafter/heldout/heldout.json --take 30
python pack_drafter.py path/to/drafter path/to/drafter-4bit
runtime_mlx/pack_dense.py writes the packed file from the fine-tuned build's dense weights, and runtime_mlx/modal_pack.py runs it on Modal. check_drafter.py compares the drafter in MLX with SpecForge's own model on reference blocks that drafter/reference.py writes.
Each test file runs on its own from the repository root, and all seven finish in under a minute.
python tests/test_frame.py
python tests/test_grammar.py
python tests/test_codes.py
python tests/test_elements.py
python tests/test_nested.py
python tests/test_rowgroup_alloc.py
python tests/test_reallocation.py
runtime_mlx/test_core.py checks the runtime against the quantizer's math. Run python runtime_mlx/make_ref.py first, which writes 1.6 GB of reference files.
runtime_mlx/test_fast_q35.py and runtime_mlx/test_block_q35.py check the Qwen3.5 kernels against NumPy products of independently unpacked codes, with no model. When LAMQ_Q35_PACKED names a packed file, the second also checks a block of tokens against decoding them one at a time. When LAMQ_Q38_PACKED names the packed Qwen3.8-27B file, it runs the same checks on a model whose output head is stored apart from its embedding.
- Model weights. Everything downloads from Hugging Face. The Qwen3.5-4B jobs expect that model in models/Qwen3.5-4B on the volume.
- Benchmark copies. eval_tasks.py downloads its benchmarks. On an offline machine it reads exported copies from data/bench/, which are not here. The Modal images run offline, so cache the benchmarks on the volume first.
- The script that assembled site/data.json. It was a set of one-off steps. The source numbers are all in results/.
- LookAtMyQuant 2's dense weights and result files. The 1.2 GB file is a release in the packed format. The dense weights the benchmarks ran on are not published, and neither are the per-problem results behind site2/data.json or the script that writes the page's numbers from them.
- The drafter at 16 bits. The drafter as trained is 1.8 GB and is not published. The 4-bit one is a release file, and modal_drafter.py trains it.

modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs lm2Math,lm2Math216,lm2TextKL
lm2BuildSmoke, lm2FinetuneSmoke and lm2MathSmoke run the same code on two layers, 60 steps and 36 problems, in a few minutes each.
The row plan. lm2Build reads results/compiler/rows/q35_rowplan_1p2GB.json, which gives 1 to 4 bits to each of 1,520 row groups. row_allocation.py made it. Its build-control command builds and packs a control with a fixed split of rows, prepare-rows allocates rows inside the control's packed bytes with rowgroup_alloc.py, and build-rows builds under the plan and checks the packed size against it.
The codebooks. The four e-means codebooks the build decodes with are in cache/codes/. --trellis-fit lloyd gives the earlier ones, and a plan made before the change keeps them unless the flag says otherwise.
Recent equal-byte recipe improvements. Two zero-byte additions are available in the compiler and fine-tuning scripts:
- Softmax-Fisher token table ranking (--embed-order sfisher-mix in compiler.py measure and quantize.py). Ranks tied embedding/output buckets by a 50/50 blend of output Softmax-Fisher E[p_v (1 - p_v)] and corpus frequency (and untied output heads by Softmax-Fisher alone).
- Mid-training equal-byte row reallocation (--realloc-at 1000 in finetune.py). Paired with --rebuild-at 1000, measures each layer's continuous-vs-stored proximal KL gap KL(all P) - KL(layer = W) to shift bits toward deep unabsorbed layers while keeping exact file size (--realloc-max-delta 1). Combined with sfisher-mix on Qwen3.5-4B at identical 1.23 GB (2.297 bpp), WikiText-2 KL fell from 0.425 to 0.387 (−9.0%) and 13-source KL from 0.447 to 0.378 (−15.5%, improving all 13 sources).
Where these jobs differ from our run. Our GSM8K solutions were written earlier, with thinking always off. Our text KL on 13 sources used 52 windows picked from the text mix by a separate tool; --sources mix takes the mix's own evaluation windows, on which our file scores 0.44 where the page reports 0.45.
run_batch.py names every job, and modal_app.py runs them. modal_fetch.py downloads models and the text mix used for calibration and evaluation.
modal run modal_app.py::setup
modal run --detach modal_fetch.py --text
modal run --detach modal_fetch.py --models google/gemma-4-E2B-it
modal run --detach modal_app.py --jobs ccMG4E2Noise,ccMG4E2Spot,ccMG4E2EmbKv
The runs behind the site used these machines, each with 8 CPU cores:
gguf_compiler.py makes plans out of llama.cpp's own tensor types, so the result runs in llama.cpp as it is. public_eval.py scores public quantizations the same way as our plans. Both run inside the image in modal_gguf.py, which builds llama.cpp with CUDA.
quantize.py --export <folder> writes a plan in the format runtime_mlx/ reads. A plan with trellis codes also needs --trellis-rows 16,8, which runs each trellis path along a row of its matrix, so that decoding an entry reads only that row. On Apple silicon, pip install . installs mlx and mlx-lm; requirements.txt holds the PyTorch compiler dependencies.
On Gemma 4 E2B at 2.5 GB, the trellis plan has 26% lower KL than the Lloyd–Max plan and decodes about 22% slower. runtime_mlx/test_trellis_gen.py and runtime_mlx/test_trellis.py check the trellis kernels against the matrices the quantizer built.
python runtime_mlx/bench.py lamq path/to/export
runtime_mlx/fast_q35.py decodes the packed 1.2 GB Qwen3.5-4B file one token at a time, straight from its trellis codes. runtime_mlx/drafter_q35.py adds speculative decoding. A small drafter proposes the seven tokens after the model's latest one in a single pass. The model then checks all eight positions in one pass over its coded weights (runtime_mlx/block_q35.py) and keeps the proposals that match its own choice, so the text is exactly its own greedy text.
On an M5 Max, over 30 held-out prompts of up to 256 tokens, generation with the drafter was 2.0 times as fast: 176 tokens a second against 88, timed in alternation in one process on 2026-11-11. A check kept 4.3 tokens on average. Over eight everyday prompts it was 1.5 times as fast. The gain follows how predictable the text is: 2.5 times on algebra, 2.0 on code, 1.2 on chat and 1.05 on a story, and one of the 30 held-out prompts ran slower with the drafter. The text matched plain greedy decoding on every prompt. These are speeds after the first token; reading the prompt takes about 0.1 s either way.
Two later changes are in this code. Each was measured against the version before it on 40 paired generations: proposing from the 96,033 most common tokens was 6.0% faster, and picking the winning token inside the packed product was 5.3% faster. Linear mode also enables self-calibrating adaptive fallback by default (adaptive=True; pass --no-adaptive to disable): when rolling acceptance drops below the measured break-even ratio of drafted-check latency to serial-step latency (accounting for cache flush and drafter context replay), it pauses drafting for short exact serial bursts and resumes once acceptance recovers, preserving bit-exact greedy parity while avoiding slowdowns on low-acceptance prose.
The drafter is a DSpine model, trained with SpecForge from the published DFlash drafter for Qwen3.5-4B on the 1.2 GB file's own greedy answers. modal_drafter.py runs the training as one Modal job, and it cost $42. On 300 held-out prompts the trained drafter gets 4.65 tokens per check, against 4.30 for the stock DFlash drafter.
pack_drafter.py stores the drafter at 4 bits a weight in the build's own code, with no calibration data: 464 MB against 1.83 GB at 16 bits. On 60 held-out prompts the 4-bit drafter kept 4.35 tokens per check against 4.38, and the text was the same. drafter_q35.py loads either form and writes the packed one out dense as it loads. The 4-bit drafter is the lm2-drafter-4bit release. It runs beside the packed 1.2 GB file, the lm2-qwen35-4b-1.2gb release.
gh release download lm2-qwen35-4b-1.2gb --repo ShinMegamiBoson/lookatmyquant -D q35-1.2gb
gh release download lm2-drafter-4bit --repo ShinMegamiBoson/lookatmyquant -D drafter-4bit
python runtime_mlx/bench_drafted.py q35-1.2gb drafter-4bit
The benchmark defaults to linear verification. Use --mode chat-fork to try a second candidate at an ambiguous early position, or --mode copy-wide for known contiguous copies. Copy mode uses the source cursor and tunes the gate/up and down projections for 16-slot checks. Both modes verify all emitted tokens with the packed target. To benchmark the chat fork, run:
python runtime_mlx/bench_drafted.py q35-1.2gb drafter-4bit --mode chat-fork --prompts chat
For a copy benchmark, pass --mode copy-wide --heldout path/to/copy-prompts.json. The JSON entries carry source, prompt_len, and input_ids from the packed model's tokenizer.
The checks, and the packer for a drafter of your own:
python runtime_mlx/test_fast_q35.py
LAMQ_Q35_PACKED=path/to/packed python runtime_mlx/test_block_q35.py
python runtime_mlx/check_drafter.py path/to/packed path/to/drafter
python runtime_mlx/bench_drafted.py path/to/packed path/to/drafter --heldout path/to/drafter/heldout/heldout.json --take 30
python pack_drafter.py path/to/drafter path/to/drafter-4bit
runtime_mlx/pack_dense.py writes the packed file from the fine-tuned build's dense weights, and runtime_mlx/modal_pack.py runs it on Modal. check_drafter.py compares the drafter in MLX with SpecForge's own model on reference blocks that drafter/reference.py writes.
Each test file runs on its own from the repository root, and all seven finish in under a minute.
python tests/test_frame.py
python tests/test_grammar.py
python tests/test_codes.py
python tests/test_elements.py
python tests/test_nested.py
python tests/test_rowgroup_alloc.py
python tests/test_reallocation.py
runtime_mlx/test_core.py checks the runtime against the quantizer's math. Run python runtime_mlx/make_ref.py first, which writes 1.6 GB of reference files.
runtime_mlx/test_fast_q35.py and runtime_mlx/test_block_q35.py check the Qwen3.5 kernels against NumPy products of independently unpacked codes, with no model. When LAMQ_Q35_PACKED names a packed file, the second also checks a block of tokens against decoding them one at a time. When LAMQ_Q38_PACKED names the packed Qwen3.8-27B file, it runs the same checks on a model whose output head is stored apart from its embedding.
- Model weights. Everything downloads from Hugging Face. The Qwen3.5-4B jobs expect that model in models/Qwen3.5-4B on the volume.
- Benchmark copies. eval_tasks.py downloads its benchmarks. On an offline machine it reads exported copies from data/bench/, which are not here. The Modal images run offline, so cache the benchmarks on the volume first.
- The script that assembled site/data.json. It was a set of one-off steps. The source numbers are all in results/.
- LookAtMyQuant 2's dense weights and result files. The 1.2 GB file is a release in the packed format. The dense weights the benchmarks ran on are not published, and neither are the per-problem results behind site2/data.json or the script that writes the page's numbers from them.
- The drafter at 16 bits. The drafter as trained is 1.8 GB and is not published. The 4-bit one is a release file, and modal_drafter.py trains it.

Nvidia's Accounting Practices Now Being Investigated by America's SEC
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Nvidia's Accounting Practices Now Being Investigated by America's SEC (reuters.com) 12
			
		 	
				We knew America's Securities and Exchange Commission was investigating Nvidia.
But Reuters reported Friday that what's being investigated is Nvidia's accounting practices, according to "two people briefed on the matter."
Prices of Nvidia shares extended losses and were down about 13% on Friday, according to LSEG data, after Reuters reported details of the SEC probe. They had been down more than 6% before Reuters published its report... The SEC probe is the latest of Nvidia's legal woes. The Justice Department has also been reviewing Nvidia's $12.9-billion deal to buy open-source platform Hugging Face alongside a broader antitrust investigation into the company's acquisitions and practices, Reuters reported last month. 
Reuters says they "could not immediately determine" exactly what potential accounting issues America's SEC is examining."  (And Reuters points out that "Investigations are not evidence of wrongdoing and many close without resulting in enforcement action.")
Their article adds some historical context — that U.S. authorities "have been investigating Megaspeed since 2024 over possible violations of export controls related to alleged shipments of Nvidia AI chips to China, the US Justice and Commerce departments said this week." And the Financial Times reported Tuesday that Nvidia removed Singapore from its geographical breakdown of revenue sources, Reuters points out, "even as Singapore sales soared to almost half its global revenue."
Analysts and investors have also grown increasingly concerned about circular AI financing deals, in which chipmakers including Nvidia have pursued complex financial arrangements to help customers fund purchases of their chips and the construction of costly AI infrastructure, such as data centers...  Speaking to Reuters last month, SEC enforcement chief David Woodcock said the agency was examining AI companies' corporate disclosures to ensure issuers are giving investors the full picture on potential risks. On its Tuesday earnings call, Nvidia's interim CEO and former CFO Colette Kress said the company was proud of its role in supporting the AI industry, but would be far more selective moving forward about AI industry financing deals.

The FBI and USDA were reportedly hacked
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
17:42:27 UTC
02:42:27 KST

Friday, it was reported that both the US Department of Agriculture (USDA) and the FBI were hacked. In reporting on the FBI hack, CNN noted that this is the second such attack on the agency in the last few months, this time targeting the National Crime Information Center (NCIC), which federal, state, and local law enforcement use to share information, including warrants and missing persons. President Donald Trump later confirmed the hack in an interview with Fox News. NBC first reported the USDA hack on Friday. According to Reuters:
The FBI and USDA were reportedly hacked
The list of hacks hitting the US government over the last few months keeps growing.
While the payments database is the known extent of the intrusion so far, the former officials warned that the compromised system could provide a backdoor to more sensitive information, including food safety data and research on animal-borne illnesses, according to the report.
The breaches come following a week of major outages across several sectors, including at the FAA and NSA, which the government has sought to reassure the public were not the result of a cyberattack.
President Trump suggested that the breach of the FBI might not be an entirely bad thing, telling Fox News that, “Maybe we’ll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place,” and “wouldn’t it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?” referring to investigations into his ties to Russia.
The FBI suffered a breach, reported in late September, that allegedly compromised personal records of “all FBI employees and applicants.” This was followed by a hack of DHS, reported in October, comprising 40,000 employee records. In the wake of these major outages, the White House national cyber director, Sean Cairncross, stepped down this week.
Update November 21st: Added President Trump’s comments.

PostgreSQL Extensions in Go
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
17:08:04 UTC
02:08:04 KST

PostgreSQL extensions in Go
gopgext builds PostgreSQL extensions from plain Go code, using TinyGo. Mark a function with //gopgext:function and it becomes a SQL function: gopgext writes the C glue, the install script and the control file.
Just Go
A project is plain Go files. The standard library works: fmt, regexp, encoding/json, crypto/*, maps, generics, iterators, defer/recover.
Types map to SQL
Strings, numbers, arrays, NULLs, sets, rows, timestamps, UUIDs and JSON map to SQL types. Define your own data types and composite types in Go.
Queries and errors
Run SQL through SPI, return errors with SQLSTATE codes, log, read settings and stay responsive to query cancellation.
Beyond functions
Write triggers, aggregates, GiST, GIN, SP-GiST and BRIN operator classes, index access methods, foreign data wrappers and query hooks.
Proven on contrib
Ports of PostgreSQL’s contrib modules (pgcrypto, hstore, pg_stat_statements, cube, …) pass upstream’s regression tests.
Safe coexistence
Several Go extensions load in one backend. PostgreSQL errors never unwind through Go frames, and results are copied into PostgreSQL memory.
Hello, world#
package main
import (
	"strings"
	"gopgext/pg"
)
// Hello returns a greeting for name.
//
//gopgext:function immutable parallel=safe
func Hello(name string) string {
	return "Hello, " + name + "!"
}
// Shout upper-cases s. An empty string raises an error.
//
//gopgext:function immutable parallel=safe
func Shout(s string) (string, error) {
	if s == "" {
		return "", pg.Errorf(pg.CodeInvalidParameterValue, "nothing to shout")
	}
	return strings.ToUpper(s) + "!", nil
}
func main() {} // runs once per backend, when PostgreSQL loads the library
gopgext init hello       # creates hello/go.mod and hello/main.go
gopgext install hello    # builds hello.so, hello.control, hello--1.0.sql and installs them
psql -c 'CREATE EXTENSION hello' -c "SELECT hello('world')"
#     hello
# ---------------
#  Hello, world!
The tutorial walks through it step by step.
A sibling project#
gopgext uses the same approach as gokm, which builds Linux kernel modules from Go: a custom TinyGo target and runtime layer for the host, a C runtime compiled by the host’s own build system (PGXS here, kbuild there), and generated glue.

Is selling an AI version of your face to this microdrama startup the future of Hollywood?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

For the most part, the content offered by the microdrama startup Shortical is indistinguishable from the rest of its industry. The company produces soapy, melodramatic storylines — almost always risqué romances — that, in bite-sized two-minute episodes, compel viewers to stay glued to their phones so as not to miss the latest twist in a tawdry, cliff-hanger-happy saga.
The primary way that Shortical — which was founded last January — makes money is through subscriptions. Typically, the first 7-10 episodes of a micro-drama are free. After that, if the user wants to finish the series, they have to pay a small monthly fee which unlocks access to all of the site’s content.
Over the past year, the company has churned out a gush of popular live-action romances like “Crossing the Line with My Neighbor,” “Accidentally Married My Boss,” and “Spoiled by My Billionaire Sugar Daddy.”
Lately, however, the company’s homepage is covered in AI-generated content. The reason isn’t exactly hard to figure out: the production overhead for AI content is substantially less than live-action. The profit potential, meanwhile, is substantially higher, if you can draw an audience.
Its latest microdrama premiered in Los Angeles last week. The series, called “Inevitable,” stars Aki Avni, an Israeli film and television star who has been visible in American TV shows for years (he had a stint on Fox’s 9/11-era thriller “24” years ago) but is now trying something largely unheard of in modern Hollywood: licensing his face to Shortical for the sake of its AI content.
The project, which is wholly AI, used Avni’s face to create a digital avatar of him. In “Inevitable,” he plays himself – in a very meta plot in which he — the actor Aki Avni — licenses his face to a film studio, after which thriller-like antics ensue.
This is Shortical’s first foray into such a licensing deal, but it likely won’t be their last.
In a phone interview, Avni told TechCrunch that he found the project both nerveracking and exciting. “Look, it’s a new era for artists,” the actor said. “AI is a new kind of art. It’s a new format. It’s always great to be the pioneer in something.”
Guy Shimoni, the CEO of Shortical, emphasized the creative potential of the new tool. “One of the things that AI does is it enables smaller teams to be able to produce these movie-scale productions,” Shimoni said. “When we were doing mobile-first live action, we were limited by budget, but now we can serve them with epic fantasies. We have kingdoms and dragons. We have superheroes. We have werewolves.”
But AI-generated romances involving werewolves — who, exactly, is watching this kind of thing?
Apparently, a lot of people. While China boasts the biggest microdrama audience globally, Shimoni says that most of his platform’s viewers are actually in the U.S. The industry’s viewer demographic skews heavily female. Many viewers may watch them to wind down at night, getting minor dopamine hits as they drift off to sleep. Many of the AI-generated programs that already exist on the company’s website appear to have millions of views.
Now, Shortical is betting on the creation of a new generation of creators — those who, like Avni, are willing to surrender their likenesses for compensation. “LICENSE YOUR FACE” Shortical’s website advertises, explaining that interested parties can submit their social media handles to the company, which will then decide whether to offer them “a contract setting out the terms” of using their persona in the company’s AI-generated content.
A company rep told TechCrunch that this “offering is still in its early days and hasn’t yet resulted in any signed contracts.” They added: “The series with Aki is the first time Shortical has used a real person’s likeness, and it was done through a direct agreement with him rather than through the platform. Shortical hopes the series will draw more attention to face licensing and plans to promote the offering more actively following this launch.”
Much of this trend has already taken hold in China where the microdrama industry originated, where AI-generated content is big, and where face-licensing is already a trend. Although the trend may not be a particularly profitable one for creators. A recent report in Rest of World found that Microdrama firms were paying people “between US$15 to US$700 to license their facial likenesses.”
How much money is Shortical offering for its face-licensing deals? The company isn’t saying. As for if Shortical will offer royalty-based compensation for creators — like the residual system in television, “It’s a case by case [basis],” Shimoni answers vaguely.
A Shortical representative further explained to TechCrunch that the company didn’t want to “share a specific figure” for Avni’s particular contract. However, the person said, he came “on as a partner in the project, and his compensation was in line with industry standards for a comparable non-AI acting role.”
For other actors, however, the deals may be quite different. Plus, many creatives are understandably unsettled by signs of automation creeping into their industry. Until recently, microdramas seemed like a safe, entry-level gateway into the entertainment industry, where young actors could gain experience on sets and hone their craft. Now, companies are downsizing and producing content that doesn’t even require real actors.
Jennifer Cooper, a longtime fan of the industry and founder of the consultancy firm Vertical Drama Love, notes that there’s a lot of distrust of AI — both from audiences and within the microdrama industry itself.
“Everyone’s been impacted by AI,” she said. “A lot of us have either lost work to AI or can see that it’s devastated this industry — [taking microdramas] from something that was a rare thing in the world, that was in growth, and providing work, particularly for young people…[and now] you know, it’s been decimated.”
Questions of consent with AI likenesses have also dogged the industry (some creators claim they have had their likenesses stolen), which is something that Shortical is obviously trying to address with its new face-licensing endeavor.
Yet these licensing deals could also help to gig-ify the industry, creating a scenario in which creators pay a distributor for the chance to platform their content (or their face), then receive payouts based on the audience engagement they’re allowed to generate. Shortical recently partnered with a company called Payouts.com, which describes itself as a “payout infrastructure platform powering creator and gig economy disbursements at scale.” The plan is to create a system by which creatives can seamlessly interact with Shortical’s platform and get paid.
Cooper believes the push towards AI in the industry is driven by broader financial trends. Most of the microdrama business is led by startups, and VCs love to hear a growth game plan involving automation. “For anyone to get investment at the moment in microdramas, they’ve got to mention the word AI,” she says. “It’s like AI equals gold in everyone’s eyes.”
Despite all the investment in this space, Cooper seems skeptical that there is actually a huge audience for this kind of content. “Some of it does get views. A lot of it doesn’t,” she said, of the AI content. “I think it’s being pushed on an audience more than there is an audience demand or wish for it.”

Verification files for the Lean proof of the Riemann Hypothesis
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
15:54:34 UTC
00:54:34 KST

Verification files for the Lean proof of the Riemann Hypothesis
Announced at alpo.ge/remark.pdf on 20 November 2026.
Andrew Sutherland (MIT), checks run with Claude Code. Page last updated 2026-11-21 12:55 UTC. Results below are as of that time and this page will be updated as the remaining checks finish.
Summary
The published Lean source compiles without errors, the five headline theorems depend only on the three standard axioms, every module has been replayed through the Lean kernel, two independently written kernels, nanoda and con-leche, accept the full exported proof, and lean4lean accepts every compiled module. con-leche's --compare mode, an independent implementation of Comparator's statement matching, confirms all five theorems against the challenge. The lake comparator run and the Lean-kernel replay of the export are still in progress.
What was verified
Five theorems, proved in the submission's Main.lean. Their statements are below exactly as they appear in the challenge file, which imports only Mathlib. All the mathematical notions in them (riemannZeta, DirichletCharacter, DirichletCharacter.LFunction, IsPrimitive) are Mathlib's.
1. The Riemann Hypothesis, using Mathlib's own definition (Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/RiemannZeta.lean): every zero of ζ other than the trivial zeros −2, −4, −6, … has real part 1/2. The condition s ≠ 1 is there because Mathlib assigns ζ a conventional value at its pole.
def RiemannHypothesis : Prop :=
  ∀ (s : ℂ) (_ : riemannZeta s = 0) (_ : ¬∃ n : ℕ, s = -2 * (n + 1)) (_ : s ≠ 1), s.re = 1 / 2
theorem riemannHypothesis : RiemannHypothesis
2. The same statement written out in the submission's own words. Lean accepts the two as equal by definition.
theorem riemannZeta_eq_zero_re :
  ∀ s : ℂ, riemannZeta s = 0 → (¬∃ n : ℕ, s = -2 * ((n : ℂ) + 1)) → s ≠ 1 → s.re = 1 / 2
3. No zeros to the right of the critical line, for every Dirichlet L-function. For every modulus N ≥ 1 and every Dirichlet character χ modulo N, primitive or not, L(χ, s) ≠ 0 whenever Re s > 1/2. The one excluded case is the trivial character at s = 1, where the L-function has its pole.
theorem LFunction_ne_zero_of_half_lt_re :
  ∀ (N : ℕ) [NeZero N] (χ : DirichletCharacter ℂ N) (s : ℂ),
    1 / 2 < s.re → (χ ≠ 1 ∨ s ≠ 1) → DirichletCharacter.LFunction χ s ≠ 0
4. Zeros in the critical strip lie on the critical line, for every Dirichlet L-function.
theorem LFunction_eq_zero_re :
  ∀ (N : ℕ) [NeZero N] (χ : DirichletCharacter ℂ N) (s : ℂ),
    0 < s.re → s.re < 1 → DirichletCharacter.LFunction χ s = 0 → s.re = 1 / 2
5. The same for primitive characters, the form in which the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis for Dirichlet L-functions is usually stated.
theorem LFunction_eq_zero_re_of_isPrimitive :
  ∀ (N : ℕ) [NeZero N] (χ : DirichletCharacter ℂ N), χ.IsPrimitive →
    ∀ s : ℂ, 0 < s.re → s.re < 1 → DirichletCharacter.LFunction χ s = 0 → s.re = 1 / 2
Scope. "GRH" here means Dirichlet L-functions: all moduli and all characters. Dedekind zeta functions, Hecke L-functions and automorphic L-functions in general are not among the statements that were checked.
Arithmetic consequences
As a check that does not depend on Mathlib's definition of the zeta function, the proof was combined with kimihiro64/Robin1984 (commit acab1a3), an independent formalization of Robin's and Lagarias's criteria on the same Lean and Mathlib versions. Importing both gives two theorems about the divisor sum, with no hypotheses and no mention of zeta (ArithSolution.lean; statements alone in ArithChallenge.lean, which imports only Mathlib):
theorem robin_inequality :
    ∀ n : ℕ, 5040 < n →
      ((ArithmeticFunction.sigma 1 n : ℕ) : ℝ) <
        Real.exp Real.eulerMascheroniConstant * (n : ℝ) * Real.log (Real.log (n : ℝ))
theorem lagarias_inequality :
    ∀ n : ℕ, 0 < n →
      ((ArithmeticFunction.sigma 1 n : ℕ) : ℝ) ≤
        (harmonic n : ℝ) + Real.exp (harmonic n : ℝ) * Real.log (harmonic n : ℝ)
The combined export is 61,752,134,938 bytes, sha256 44651dcc482a6e06e8d626994221aff638e422fded189bf856e0c31176926f0d; its challenge export has sha256 088a57c9e7454fa5279a86a825e551828ac8431694855aadd1f6e6a6eb37bb1d. These statements still rely on Mathlib's definitions of the divisor sum, Euler's constant, harmonic numbers, exp and log, and on the Robin1984 proof, which is why that proof was put through the same checks.
Downloads
The exported proof: output of lean4export on module Main. Unpacks to 61,570,595,788 bytes.
The exported challenge: the five statements with sorry proofs, importing only Mathlib. Unpacks to 350,862,970 bytes.
Smaller files:
- comparator.json: Comparator config naming the checkers that can process this proof
- SHA256SUMS: hashes of everything on this page
- audit.log and Audit.lean: the axiom audit and the file that produced it
- project-oleans.sha256: sha256 of each of the 175,187 compiled files (29 MB). These hashes depend on the build directory; see the note below.
- nanoda.log: timing and exit status of the nanoda run
- con-leche-master.log and con-leche-compare.log: the two con-leche runs
Check it yourself
With Lean v4.36.0 installed, nothing needs to be built. You need roughly 100 GB of RAM and 70 GB of disk.
zstd -d --long=30 solution.ndjson.zst      # --long=30 is required
zstd -d --long=27 challenge.ndjson.zst
sha256sum solution.ndjson challenge.ndjson  # compare with the hashes above
lake comparator --config comparator.json \
  --challenge-from-export challenge.ndjson --solution-from-export solution.ndjson
nanoda alone took about 28 minutes on 80 threads. The Lean kernels are single-threaded and take much longer.
The fastest independent check is con-leche built from source (Lean v4.36.0, about two minutes to build). It needs about 50 GB of RAM and took under four minutes on 64 threads:
git clone https://github.com/leanprover/con-leche && cd con-leche
git checkout compare        # 5dc36dc1; master (cd2eceab) for the plain check
lake build con-leche
.lake/build/bin/con-leche --compare challenge.ndjson solution.ndjson
Provenance
Caveats
- The build ran on cloud machines without a sandbox, and compiled each file by calling lean directly with the same inputs lake supplies (module name, package name and the lakefile's options), not through lake build.
- The per-file .olean hashes depend on the build directory. In Lean v4.33.1 a compiled file stores each linter message together with the absolute path of its source file. Of our 175,187 compiled files, 98,219 contain the path /home/ubuntu/grh/src/lean/src/…; the other 76,968, including Main.olean, contain no path. So project-oleans.sha256 and its sha256 can be reproduced in full only by a build whose source tree is at /home/ubuntu/grh/src/lean. For a build at any other path, only the files of modules that emit no linter messages should match.
What we checked: alake build of 240 modules on a different machine, at a different path, gave 168 files identical to the manifest and 72 different; all 72 contain that build's path and none of the 168 do. Separately, 77 modules compiled both ways in the same directory, by lake build and by our direct invocation, were byte-identical. We have not compared the full set against a lake build.
- The exported proof (solution.ndjson) is the artifact the independent kernels checked. Its hash is the one to compare across builds; we have not examined whether the export format can carry a source path.
- The export was produced from that unsandboxed build. We have not ourselves compared its sha256 with an export made from a sandboxed lake build.
- A passing check shows that the formal statements, as written in Lean against Mathlib's definitions, follow from the three standard axioms according to the kernels used. It relies on those kernels and on Mathlib's definitions being what they are intended to be.

====================================================================================================
