The game of the year is finally here
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
02:53:15 UTC
11:53:15 KST

Hi, friends! Welcome to Installer No. 153, your guide to the best and Verge-iest stuff in the world. (If you’re new here, welcome, thank you for tearing yourself away from GTA to be here, and also you can read all the old editions at the Installer homepage.)
The game of the year is finally here
Plus, in this week’s Installer: A better YouTube Kids, new ways to get stuff done, the FTX story on Netflix, and more.
This week, I’ve been reading about nuclear radiation and F1 brains and phone scammers, watching about 10 minutes of Madden before bailing, playing a lot of Madden NFL instead, learning my way around Instagram Edits on the Mac, trying the 12-3-30 workout while watching Mission: Impossible movies in half-hour increments, getting my new Apple HomeView up and running (still don’t know why I bought one…), and dreaming up stuff to do with the Flipper One.
I also have for you the game of the year (decade? century?), a couple of great new productivity tools, a Netflix take on the FTX story, and much more. But mostly, it’s GTA week. Let’s do it.
Also, programming note: Installer is off next week. It’s Thanksgiving, there’s food to eat and football to watch. Hope you have a great couple of weeks, and we’ll be so back in December.
(As always, the best part of Installer is your ideas and tips. What have you been watching / reading / listening to / playing / adding to your holiday lists this week? Tell me everything: installer@theverge.com. And if you know someone else who might enjoy Installer, forward it to them and tell them to subscribe here.)
The Drop
- Grand Theft Auto VI. I mean, this literally could be the whole newsletter. But it appears this game is everything we’ve been waiting for, and I anticipate spending too much of the rest of my life playing it. No regrets.
- The GTA VI soundtrack. I can’t think of many games whose soundtracks have this kind of power: It’s a 34-song compilation, including a bunch of new music from huge artists. I’m going to listen to this while I work, which I assume will make all my writing cooler and more crime-filled.
- The Teenage Engineering EP/BC-1. I’ve been thinking about rebooting my physical media collection, and I can’t think of a better way to start than with this beautiful CD player. Like all Teenage Engineering gear, it is somehow retro and futuristic at the same time, and it’ll slide neatly into a synth setup or just look extremely rad in your living room.
- Alfred 6. A long-awaited update to the launcher and workflow tool so many Mac users love. I see some nice interface changes here but otherwise not a massively different product… but that’s actually probably the right call. Alfred is very good at being Alfred.
- The TRMNL Frame. A 31.5-inch, programmable color E Ink screen that you can hang on your wall. This either immediately excites you, and $1,299 is a small price to pay, or does nothing for you and is way too expensive. I accept both reactions equally. I also want one so badly.
- Tasks in Gemini. A stupid, stupid, stupid name for an otherwise useful product! Gemini now has a lot of the same “do stuff for you” tools that Muse and Dots offer — this time with much better access to your Google data. I’m slowly working through my inbox with this one.
- Who Wants This? Fun new podcast in which two writers and comedians attempt to make sense of current trends, and whether anyone actually… wants this. The first episode is about protein and proteinmaxxing, and I both enjoyed the hang and learned a lot. It also made me feel like everyone has just completely lost their minds.
- The Altruists. We are due for many, many fictionalized versions of the FTX story. I’ve heard and read some mixed things about this new Netflix series, but I dig the idea of trying to play it as both a love story and a scam story. It really is both those things!
- ClockwiseTV. Respect to Hank Green for being one of the few people bold enough to try and compete with YouTube. The pitch here is smart and simple: curated content for kids, on a timer, no ads, no autoplay. This is immediately going on all my iPads.
- “CIA Meeting Cold Open.” Last week’s SNL was really good overall (though maybe I just love John Mulaney?) but the cold open, with the tech billionaires learning how not to fall for a honeypot, absolutely slayed me. The spray bottle on Elon Musk makes me laugh every single time.
You may not know Tom Hovey, but I can practically guarantee you’ve seen his work. That’s because, in addition to many other cool projects, Tom’s also the artist behind those illustrated graphics of everyone’s bakes on The Great British Baking Show. His unique style has been all over that show, and my TV screen, for many years.
I found Tom’s work recently on Threads, when he went massively viral after debunking the idea that all those food drawings are AI with a video to prove how he does it. He’s been working with digital tools for a decade, he said, “but never ever AI.”
I asked Tom to share his homescreen with us to see how he might use his phone for his work, and also to see if I might learn any good GBBS spoilers. (Alas, none.) Here it is, plus some info on the apps he uses and why:
The phone: iPhone 17 Pro.
The wallpaper: A sunrise photo of the Swan River, where I run most mornings in Perth Western Australia. We moved here permanently 6 months ago.
The apps: Apple Podcasts, Brick, Sonos, Strava, SoundCloud, Photos, Google, Google Maps, iNaturalist, Google Calendar, Chrome, NYT Games, Gmail, WhatsApp, Spotify, Discord.
Like many people I’m addicted to my phone, so bricking my phone when I get home from the studio has been amazing. I’m obsessed with running, so Strava and Runna are used every day for training and logging my runs but also hyping up my running pals.
Six apps for my kids’ primary school is too many apps.
I have weather apps but it’s kinda unnecessary now we live in Australia, because it’s either hot or really hot!
iNaturalist is my newest app and I love it. It’s for documenting nature — flora and fauna. As I’m in a new country, I’m enjoying discovering all the native flowers and plants I find on runs. I’ve also recently done some trial runs in Perth Hills, where I’ve run into tons of wild kangaroos and snakes. So fun! My kids also love bugs and birds, so learning all about them is cool. Also when you add your photos of nature, it geotags them and sends the details to some scientists who can understand the areas you are documenting.
I also asked Tom to share a few things he’s into right now. Here’s what he sent back:
- I listen to a lot of audiobooks while I’m at my desk drawing and also while running, and audiobooks are the only way I can “read” non-fiction as I just fall asleep immediately if I try and actually read them. Some recent audiobooks I’ve enjoyed: The Castle by Jon Ronson; Thirst: Twelve Drinks That Changed My Life by John Robins; Hidden Valley Road by Robert Kolker; London Falling by Patrick Radden Keefe.
- TV shows I’ve recently watched: The Pitt, Widow’s Bay, This Is a Gardening Show, East of Eden, Star City, Pluribus.
- As an illustrator I’m always looking for excellent people making cool things and in a world where AI art is taking over I am seeking more and more analog art so here are some of the coolest creatives I’m currently enjoying: Natalie Andrewson, Sean Morris, Carla Adams, Ellis Parrinder, Azpeger Tat, and Kawafi.
- I have a WhatsApp chat with a bunch of my old illustrator pals which is a bunch of old men gossiping and sending each other Paul McCartney memes.
- Really enjoying the internet’s response to Damian Lewis’s foray into music.
Crowdsourced
Here’s what the Installer community is into this week. I want to know what you’re into right now as well! Email installer@theverge.com or message me on Signal — @davidpierce.11 — with your recommendations for anything and everything, and we’ll feature some of our favorites here every week. For even more great recommendations, check out the replies to this post on Threads and this post on Bluesky.
“Forget GTA VI, the game of the year is Queen’s Domain. It is only $20 instead of $80, made by two people instead of an army. It is all I have been playing this week.” — Mike
“In Meg Elison’s new novel, Foundling Fathers, some creepy tech billionaires clone Washington Jefferson, Franklin, and Adams and try to raise them in an ‘originalist’ setting. The satire is hilarious and all too plausible.” — aAron
“I’m not playing with it yet myself, but the FM-1 from M-Vave seems to be capturing the first wave of post-vibe-coding synthesized nerds, who are all replacing the stock firmware with homebrew. So a cheap Chinese generic keyboard is suddenly really hot hardware even though it’s like $50.” — Anil
“Reading John Scalzi’s The Kaiju Preservation Society and it is simple, honest sci-fi fun. Classic Scalzi.” — 4nd3rs
“Stick on Apple TV got me through the week until GTA VI!” — Justin
“Claude and I went around this week with a tape measure and built a to-the-inch accurate 3D model and floorplan of our house, which inevitably led to making it a Doom map.” — Michael
“I switched to ReadKit on my Mac and iPhone now that it has iCloud-based RSS sync.” — Todd
“Recently found the book The Canceling of the American Mind by Greg Lukianoff and Rikki Schlott at my local library. It is a well-written investigation into the effects of cancel culture on public debate and education. Wherever you fall on the political spectrum, this book will challenge you to think about how we should engage with ‘dangerous’ and ‘offensive’ ideas.” — Josh
“Been watching From since the first three seasons are available on Netflix right now. It’s a good show, has very Lost vibes to it.” — Bobby
Signing off
One of the most underrated strange things about the tech industry is how much The Social Network changed the way people think about Mark Zuckerberg. Yes, the movie takes huge liberties, and yes, every single person involved with the movie was quick to remind us that it was fiction. But for so many people, Jesse Eisenberg’s character just became who Zuck was.
I think Andrew Garfield in Artificial might be about to do the same thing to Sam Altman. The new trailer looks fabulous, all the early buzz about the movie seems to be great, and the movie absolutely could not be more perfectly timed. It’s apparently a pretty brutal look at Altman, OpenAI, and the whole AI movement — I wonder how Garfield and his handsomeness might bend the way the world responds. I cannot wait to see this movie.
See you in two weeks!

Clojure will affect the way you think about programming (2017)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
02:40:39 UTC
11:40:39 KST

I sometimes get asked for recommendations of what programming language to "learn next". It's great that folks appreciate the fact that being exposed to several sufficiently different programming languages is an important step on the path to programming enlightenment. But it's also true that, given limited free time, the choice of which languages to learn is important. As the famous quote by Alan Perlis says:
A language that doesn't affect the way you think about programming, is not
worth knowing.
In this post I want to explain why I think Clojure is a terrific language for this task. Whatever your background, it's almost guaranteed to affect the way you think about programming. It's a fairly new language (from 2007) that did a good job of collecting insights and paradigms from many existing languages and organizing them into a coherent whole. It's chock-full of great ideas. In other words, the perfect language to expand your brain.
It's a Lisp!
First and foremost, Clojure belongs to the Lisp family of languages, like Common Lisp, Scheme and Racket. Lisp is one of the oldest and still most important families of programming languages to be familiar with. Being a Lisp in this case means several things:
- Clojure is a dynamic language. No matter where you stand on the static vs. dynamic typing debate, knowing languages in both camps is important. Clojure has a kind of optional typing, but in essence it's dynamic.
- It promotes combinations of built-in data structures (lists, maps, vectors) over objects and has very good reasons for doing so.
- It promotes programming with higher-order functions. Some built-in features like reducers and transducers rely heavily on composing higher order functions for transforming other functions.
- It has uniform syntax with full Lisp-style macro support! This capability has proved to be very elusive for non-Lisp languages, and seeing some real macros in action is enlightening. Clojure has a whole Go-like CSP equivalent implemented using macros in core.async.
- It encourages REPL-based development, where functionality is built and testsed gradually from the bottom up in an interactive terminal.
Importantly, Clojure is a very modern Lisp, with tons of libraries for tasks common in our day and age. It also builds upon important recent research in data structures, such as efficient persistent vectors, that only appeared in the 1990s or later. I've been dabbling with various Lisps for almost 15 years, and Clojure is the first Lisp I'd consider using in production.
I want the stress the importance of full Lisp-style macros once again. Lisp's (and Clojure's) uniform syntax and macros force us to think about the Meta-circular abstraction - there's just no way around it! We have to think of our code as data, and this has profound implications. Back in 2005, when I was still a programming neophyte, I faced a performance challenge for a data-decoding program I was hacking on in Perl. Having learned some Lisp, I had the insight of generating and evaluating new Perl code on the fly, which turned out to be key to the success of that program. I'm confident I could not have come up with that solution back then without being aware of the "Lisp way".
Pragmatism and hard-won experience
The designers of Clojure are extremely pragmatic, building upon decades of industry experience. I strongly recommend watching some YouTube videos of talks by leading Clojure developers. In particular, watch a few of the more popular talks by Rich Hickey - Clojure's original designer. The Alan Perlis quote mentioned above may be his most popular, but there's another I find at least as insightful:
Simplicity does not precede complexity, but follows it.
There's a certain quality in programmers I believe we can all recognize. It can only come from real, hard-won experience of building systems for many years and pondering about how to build such systems more effectively next time. Rich Hickey certainly belongs to this quality category - his talks are very insightful, and this philosophy reigns in the design and implementation of Clojure, as well as its (friendly and vibrant) community.
Sequences and laziness for powerful in-language data processing
Applications that process and extract useful bits of information from large datasets all look alike in many programming languages, at least to some extent. What we really want in many cases is SQL-like primitives built into our languages, but this is often challenging (.NET's LINQ is one example of a successful approach).
Clojure combines pervasive sequence protocols with persistent data structures and laziness to make this kind of task natural, using only built-in tools. Here's a realistic example of a function taken from the Clojure Applied book:
(defn revenue-by-department [carts]
  (->> (filter :settled? carts)
       (mapcat :line-items)
       (map line-summary)
       (group-by :dept)
       (reduce-kv dept-total)))
All the functions used here are built-ins, including the ->> macro. This code reads like SQL and it's efficient too. In fact, it can be made somewhat more efficient and even seamlessly parallelized using Reducers and Transducers.
The clojure.spec library provides a degree of type-safety for nested data structures. If you've ever written comments like "this maps strings to lists, where each list element is a map of ...", clojure.spec makes it more formal and verifiable. This is very useful when working with data, and is another example of where Clojure's pragmatism shines; the language is dynamic in its core, but when you need static checking - it's there, optionally (core.typed is another option for a more statically typed flavor).
The right approach to object-orientation
Historically, Lisp programmers weren't the biggest proponents of OOP. This doesn't mean that OOP has absolutely no value in Lisp-y languages, however. Common Lisp has had CLOS for decades, and Clojure also comes with an array of OO-like features.
That said, Clojure's flavor of OOP is particularly powerful and tends to discourage bad practices. Clojure uses "protocols", which are a kind of interfaces, and encourages thinking in terms of protocols rather than in terms of classes with inheritance hierarchies, sort of like Go. Add to that an ability to do true multiple dispatch and you have a powerful modeling tool at your fingertips.
Built-in support for concurrency and parallelism
It wouldn't be a real language of the 2000s without powerful support for concurrency and parallelism right at the core. Clojure supports this in many ways, which are interleaved.
First, it's a functional language with persistent data structures. Persistent data structures are effectively immutable, which makes them very attractive in a multi-threaded context. Clojure has a great implementation of persistent data structures even for things like vectors (which are quite challenging to implement in an efficient manner). This is a bigger deal than may originally appear. Pure functions are often great in theory but fail in practice due to too much copying of non-trivial data structures; Clojure's elegant usage of persistent data structures solves this problem, making pure functions efficient and thus much more applicable to a wide array of problems.
Second, Clojure doesn't lock itself in the closet of purism and does support lower-level primitives for concurrency and mutation, where needed. Atoms are built-in mutable state units with atomic updates. Refs and transactions go further, implementing STM.
Third, Clojure comes with a bunch of concurrency tools built in. Promises, futures and threads leverage the underlying JVM's threading utilities.
Finally, Clojure has a pretty good implementation of Go's CSP in the core.async module. It provides two useful things:
- Channels and Go select-like functionality (alts!!) which makes it easy to work with safe concurrent queues and "share data by communicating".
- go-blocks with non-blocking primitives that make it possible to write cooperative "green threads" with ease.
Conclusion - try it
Finally, it's important to mention that Clojure is far from being an academic exercise. It's used in production by several companies, and has strong roots in the massive Java ecosystem. Clojure's interoperability with Java is seamless - Java code can be called from Clojure with no special provisions, and all core Clojure entities are Java objects deep under the hood.
On the client side, Clojurescript has been gaining traction recently as another language-compiled-to-JS option. It brings the elegance of Clojure to client-side programming, among with some unique features like core.async for callback-free concurrency.
This doesn't mean that you should only consider Clojure as a fit for your next project. On the contrary, I recommend learning if even if there's little chance of using it in production any time soon. In fact, this was exactly my situation, Clojure is a great language to expand your programming horizons; who knows, maybe in the future you'll find it useful for some real work. If you do, great! If you don't, learning it will positively affect how you use other programming languages.

====================================================================================================

- It promotes programming with higher-order functions. Some built-in features like reducers and transducers rely heavily on composing higher order functions for transforming other functions.
- It has uniform syntax with full Lisp-style macro support! This capability has proved to be very elusive for non-Lisp languages, and seeing some real macros in action is enlightening. Clojure has a whole Go-like CSP equivalent implemented using macros in core.async.
- It encourages REPL-based development, where functionality is built and testsed gradually from the bottom up in an interactive terminal.
Importantly, Clojure is a very modern Lisp, with tons of libraries for tasks common in our day and age. It also builds upon important recent research in data structures, such as efficient persistent vectors, that only appeared in the 1990s or later. I've been dabbling with various Lisps for almost 15 years, and Clojure is the first Lisp I'd consider using in production.
I want the stress the importance of full Lisp-style macros once again. Lisp's (and Clojure's) uniform syntax and macros force us to think about the Meta-circular abstraction - there's just no way around it! We have to think of our code as data, and this has profound implications. Back in 2005, when I was still a programming neophyte, I faced a performance challenge for a data-decoding program I was hacking on in Perl. Having learned some Lisp, I had the insight of generating and evaluating new Perl code on the fly, which turned out to be key to the success of that program. I'm confident I could not have come up with that solution back then without being aware of the "Lisp way".
Pragmatism and hard-won experience
The designers of Clojure are extremely pragmatic, building upon decades of industry experience. I strongly recommend watching some YouTube videos of talks by leading Clojure developers. In particular, watch a few of the more popular talks by Rich Hickey - Clojure's original designer. The Alan Perlis quote mentioned above may be his most popular, but there's another I find at least as insightful:
Simplicity does not precede complexity, but follows it.
There's a certain quality in programmers I believe we can all recognize. It can only come from real, hard-won experience of building systems for many years and pondering about how to build such systems more effectively next time. Rich Hickey certainly belongs to this quality category - his talks are very insightful, and this philosophy reigns in the design and implementation of Clojure, as well as its (friendly and vibrant) community.
Sequences and laziness for powerful in-language data processing
Applications that process and extract useful bits of information from large datasets all look alike in many programming languages, at least to some extent. What we really want in many cases is SQL-like primitives built into our languages, but this is often challenging (.NET's LINQ is one example of a successful approach).
Clojure combines pervasive sequence protocols with persistent data structures and laziness to make this kind of task natural, using only built-in tools. Here's a realistic example of a function taken from the Clojure Applied book:
(defn revenue-by-department [carts]
  (->> (filter :settled? carts)
       (mapcat :line-items)
       (map line-summary)
       (group-by :dept)
       (reduce-kv dept-total)))
All the functions used here are built-ins, including the ->> macro. This code reads like SQL and it's efficient too. In fact, it can be made somewhat more efficient and even seamlessly parallelized using Reducers and Transducers.
The clojure.spec library provides a degree of type-safety for nested data structures. If you've ever written comments like "this maps strings to lists, where each list element is a map of ...", clojure.spec makes it more formal and verifiable. This is very useful when working with data, and is another example of where Clojure's pragmatism shines; the language is dynamic in its core, but when you need static checking - it's there, optionally (core.typed is another option for a more statically typed flavor).
The right approach to object-orientation
Historically, Lisp programmers weren't the biggest proponents of OOP. This doesn't mean that OOP has absolutely no value in Lisp-y languages, however. Common Lisp has had CLOS for decades, and Clojure also comes with an array of OO-like features.
That said, Clojure's flavor of OOP is particularly powerful and tends to discourage bad practices. Clojure uses "protocols", which are a kind of interfaces, and encourages thinking in terms of protocols rather than in terms of classes with inheritance hierarchies, sort of like Go. Add to that an ability to do true multiple dispatch and you have a powerful modeling tool at your fingertips.
Built-in support for concurrency and parallelism
It wouldn't be a real language of the 2000s without powerful support for concurrency and parallelism right at the core. Clojure supports this in many ways, which are interleaved.
First, it's a functional language with persistent data structures. Persistent data structures are effectively immutable, which makes them very attractive in a multi-threaded context. Clojure has a great implementation of persistent data structures even for things like vectors (which are quite challenging to implement in an efficient manner). This is a bigger deal than may originally appear. Pure functions are often great in theory but fail in practice due to too much copying of non-trivial data structures; Clojure's elegant usage of persistent data structures solves this problem, making pure functions efficient and thus much more applicable to a wide array of problems.
Second, Clojure doesn't lock itself in the closet of purism and does support lower-level primitives for concurrency and mutation, where needed. Atoms are built-in mutable state units with atomic updates. Refs and transactions go further, implementing STM.
Third, Clojure comes with a bunch of concurrency tools built in. Promises, futures and threads leverage the underlying JVM's threading utilities.
Finally, Clojure has a pretty good implementation of Go's CSP in the core.async module. It provides two useful things:
- Channels and Go select-like functionality (alts!!) which makes it easy to work with safe concurrent queues and "share data by communicating".
- go-blocks with non-blocking primitives that make it possible to write cooperative "green threads" with ease.
Conclusion - try it
Finally, it's important to mention that Clojure is far from being an academic exercise. It's used in production by several companies, and has strong roots in the massive Java ecosystem. Clojure's interoperability with Java is seamless - Java code can be called from Clojure with no special provisions, and all core Clojure entities are Java objects deep under the hood.
On the client side, Clojurescript has been gaining traction recently as another language-compiled-to-JS option. It brings the elegance of Clojure to client-side programming, among with some unique features like core.async for callback-free concurrency.
This doesn't mean that you should only consider Clojure as a fit for your next project. On the contrary, I recommend learning if even if there's little chance of using it in production any time soon. In fact, this was exactly my situation, Clojure is a great language to expand your programming horizons; who knows, maybe in the future you'll find it useful for some real work. If you do, great! If you don't, learning it will positively affect how you use other programming languages.

Teenage Engineering's compact disc player

11:39:06 KST

Teenage Engineering's compact disc player

Integrals of Polynomials with Galois Group S_p
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
01:17:05 UTC
10:17:05 KST

We investigate properties of integrals of polynomials in $Q[x]$ with Galois Group $S_p$.
The Galois group of a polynomial $f(x) \in F[x]$, is $Gal(K/F)$, where $K$ is the splitting field of $F$. We also write $Gal(K/F)$ as $Gal(f/F)$. Let $[a,b] \in \mathbb{R}$ and define \[ {I(f;[a,b])=\int_a^b f(x)\,dx} \] For a group $G$, we are interested in the collection \[ \mathcal I_{a,b}(G) = \left\{ I(f;[a,b]): \operatorname{Gal}(f)\cong G \right\}. \] Essentilly, we are interested in the values of integrals over an interval $[a,b]$ for all polynomials whose Galois group is $G$. In particular, we are interested in the case where $f(x) \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$ and $G=S_p$ for a prime p. In [1], it is shown that the following family of polynomials has Galois group isomorphic to $S_p$ for odd primes p. \[ f_p(x)=x^2(x-2)(x-4)\cdots(x-2(n-2)) - 2 \] Each member of this family is irreducible over the rationals and of degree p. We first examine whether the set $I_{a,b}(S_p)$ is bounded and then consider the existence of polynomials satisfying the Zero Integral Condition. We prove that for every prime \(p\), there exist infinitely many monic, irreducible polynomials \(f\in\mathbb Q[x]\) of degree \(p\) such that \[ \int_0^1 f(x)\,dx=0 \] and \[ \operatorname{Gal}(f/\mathbb Q)\cong S_p. \] We then generalize this result to arbitrary rational intervals.
Suppose $a,b \in \mathbb{R}$, with $a < b$. Fix a prime \(p\) and define \[ \mathcal I_{a,b}(S_p) = \left\{ \int_a^b f(x)\,dx: f\in\mathbb Q[x],\ \operatorname{Gal}(f)\cong S_p \right\}. \] Then $\mathcal I_{a,b}(S_p) $ is unbounded.
Suppose we have some polynomial \(f\) with \[ \operatorname{Gal}(f)\cong S_p \] and \[ \int_a^b f(x)\,dx=c\neq 0. \] Such a polynomial exists given the family $f_p$ mentioned earlier [1]. Consider the family of polynomials \[ f_q(x)=qf(x),\qquad q\in\mathbb Q\setminus\{0\}. \] Multiplying a polynomial by a nonzero rational number does not change its roots. Therefore, \[ \operatorname{Gal}(f_q/\mathbb Q) = \operatorname{Gal}(f/\mathbb Q) \cong S_p. \] But integration gives \[ \int_a^b f_q(x)\,dx = q\int_a^b f(x)\,dx =qc. \] Since \(q\) can be arbitrarily large or small, we see that \(\mathcal I_{a,b}(S_p)\) is unbounded. $\blacksquare$
We now consider an arbitrary interval $[a,b]$ and fixed prime $p$, and again consider polynomials $f$ with $Gal(f) \cong S_p$. We ask whether there always exists an $f$ such that it integrates to $0$ on $[a,b]$, or equivalentely, if $0 \in \mathcal I_{a,b}(S_p)$? The following theorem shows that, no, this is not the case for every interval. Note the following applies to an abitrary group $G$ thus also applies to $S_p$.
Let $G$ be a group and \[ \mathcal I_{a,b}(G) = \left\{ \int_a^b f(x)\,dx: f\in\mathbb Q[x],\ \operatorname{Gal}(f)\cong G \right\}. \] If $\mathcal I_{a,b}(G)$ is non-empty, then there exists an interval $[a,b]$ such that $0 \notin \mathcal I_{a,b}(G)$.
Suppose \[ f(x)\in\mathbb Q[x] \] and let \(F(x)\in\mathbb Q[x]\) be an antiderivative of \(f\). Then \[ \int_a^b f(x)\,dx=F(b)-F(a). \] Therefore, our condition is equivalent to \[ F(a)=F(b). \] Now choose an interval $[a,b]$ such that that \(a\) and \(b\) are algebraically independent over \(\mathbb Q\). By definition, this means that no nonzero polynomial \(P(X,Y)\in\mathbb Q[X,Y]\) satisfies \[ P(a,b)=0. \] But if our integral were zero, we would have \[ F(b)-F(a)=0. \] Consider the polynomial \[ P(X,Y)=F(Y)-F(X). \] Since \(F\) is nonconstant, \(P\) is a nonzero polynomial in \(\mathbb Q[X,Y]\). Thus, \[ P(a,b)=0 \] would contradict the algebraic independence of \(a\) and \(b\). Consequently, \[ \int_a^b f(x)\,dx\neq0 \] for every nonzero \(f\in\mathbb Q[x]\). So there exist intervals for which zero cannot belong to \(\mathcal I_{a,b}(G)\). $\blacksquare$
We now move on to an existence question and prove our main result. We will show that for every prime \(p\), there exist infinitely many monic, irreducible polynomials \(f\in\mathbb Q[x]\) of degree \(p\) such that \[ \int_0^1 f(x)\,dx=0 \] and \[ \operatorname{Gal}(f/\mathbb Q)\cong S_p. \] The key is to consider the family \[ F_{p,t}(x)=x^p+tx-\frac1{p+1}-\frac t2. \] Here \(t\) is a parameter. First, observe that \[ \begin{aligned} \int_0^1 F_{p,t}(x)\,dx &=\frac1{p+1}+\frac t2-\frac1{p+1}-\frac t2\\ &=0. \end{aligned} \] Thus every rational specialization \(t\in\mathbb Q\) automatically satisfies the integral condition. We will prove that \[ \operatorname{Gal}(F_{p,t}/\mathbb Q(t))\cong S_p. \] We can then use Hilbert's irreducibility theorem to obtain infinitely many suitable rational values of \(t\). For the rest of the proof, assume \(p\) is an odd prime. We will handle \(p=2\) separately. We prove a series of lemmas which will lead to the final proof.
\(F_{p,t}(x)\) is irreducible as a polynomial in \(x\) over the rational function field \(\mathbb Q(t)\), thus its Galois group acts transitively on its p roots.
Rewrite $F_{p,t}(x)$ as \[ F_{p,t}(x) = \underbrace{x^p-\frac1{p+1}}_{A(x)} + t\underbrace{\left(x-\frac12\right)}_{B(x)}. \] Notice that \[ \gcd(A(x),B(x))=1. \] Indeed, the only root of \(B\) is \(1/2\), and \[ A(1/2)=\frac1{2^p}-\frac1{p+1}\neq0. \] Now suppose \(F_{p,t}\) has a non-trivial factorization in \(\mathbb Q[x,t]\). Since it has degree one in \(t\), one of its factors must be independent of \(t\). That factor would have to divide both \(A(x)\) and \(B(x)\). But their greatest common divisor is \(1\). Therefore \(F_{p,t}\) is irreducible in \(\mathbb Q[x,t]\). Since it is monic in \(x\), Gauss's lemma gives $F_{p,t}$ is irreducible over $\mathbb Q(t)$. Consequently, its Galois group acts transitively on its \(p\) roots. $\blacksquare$
The permutation representation of \(Gal(F_{p,t}/Q(t))\) acting on the p roots of $F_{p,t}(x)$ contains a p-cycle.
We specialize $F_{p,t}(x)$ by letting \(t=0\). We obtain \[ F_{p,0}(x)=x^p-\frac1{p+1}. \] This polynomial is irreducible over \(\mathbb Q\). To see this, use the binomial irreducibility criterion. Since \(p\) is prime, \(x^p-c\) is irreducible unless \(c\) is a \(p\)-th power in \(\mathbb Q\). Here, \[ c=\frac1{p+1} \] is not a rational \(p\)-th power. Thus its Galois group acts transitively on its \(p\) roots [1]. By the orbit-stabilizer theorem, the order of this Galois group is divisible by \(p\). Cauchy's theorem therefore guarantees an element of order \(p\). Since we are permuting exactly \(p\) roots, this element must be a \(p\)-cycle. We can now use the specialization theorem for Galois groups, and because \(F_{p,0}\) is separable, its Galois group embeds into the generic Galois group (up to conjugacy). Therefore, \[ \operatorname{Gal}(F_{p,t}/\mathbb Q(t)) \text{ contains a }p\text{-cycle}. \] $\blacksquare$
The permutation representation of \(Gal(F_{p,t}/Q(t))\) acting on the p roots of $F_{p,t}(x)$ contains a transposition.
Recall that \[ F_{p,t}(x)=x^p+tx-\frac1{p+1}-\frac t2. \] Think of the equation \(F_{p,t}(x)=0\) as defining \(t\) in terms of \(x\): \[ t=\frac{x^p-\frac1{p+1}}{\frac12-x}. \] Define the rational function \[ R_p(x)=\frac{x^p-\frac1{p+1}}{\frac12-x}. \] The critical points of this rational function are the roots of its derivative. By the quotient rule, \[ R_p'(x)= \frac{ \frac p2x^{p-1}-(p-1)x^p-\frac1{p+1} }{ (\frac12-x)^2 }. \] Let \[ D_p(x)= \frac p2x^{p-1}-(p-1)x^p-\frac1{p+1}. \] We will show that there is a critical value of \(R_p\) corresponding to exactly one critical point, and that this critical point is simple. That will give us a transposition.
Consider \(D_p(x)\) on the negative real axis. At zero, \[ D_p(0)=-\frac1{p+1} \lt 0. \] As \(x\to-\infty\), \[ D_p(x)\to+\infty. \] Furthermore, \[ D_p'(x) = p(p-1)x^{p-2}\left(\frac12-x\right). \] Since \(p\) is odd, \(p-2\) is odd. Thus, for \(x \lt 0\), \[ D_p'(x) \lt 0. \] Consequently, \(D_p\) is strictly decreasing on the negative real axis. By the intermediate value theorem, there is exactly one negative real number \(r\) such that \[ D_p(r)=0. \] Moreover, \[ D_p'(r)\neq0, \] so \(r\) is a simple root of \(D_p\).
This is the key observation. Since \(r\) is a critical point, the derivative of \(F_{p,t}\) with respect to \(x\) vanishes when \(x=r\): \[ \frac{\partial F_{p,t}}{\partial x} = px^{p-1}+t. \] Therefore, the corresponding parameter value is \[ t_0=-pr^{p-1}. \] Now suppose there were another critical point \(s\) corresponding to the same value \(t_0\). Then \[ -ps^{p-1}=-pr^{p-1}, \] which gives \[ s^{p-1}=r^{p-1}. \] Hence we can write \[ s=\zeta r, \qquad \zeta^{p-1}=1. \] Both \(r\) and \(s\) are roots of \(D_p\). Subtracting their defining equations gives \[ 0=D_p(s)-D_p(r). \] Because \(s^{p-1}=r^{p-1}\), \[ 0=-(p-1)r^p(\zeta^p-1). \] But \[ \zeta^p=\zeta \] because \(\zeta^{p-1}=1\). Therefore, \[ \zeta=1, \] and consequently \[ s=r. \] We have proved that \(r\) is the only critical point lying above the critical value \(t_0\).
At \(t=t_0\), the polynomial \(F_{p,t_0}(x)\) has a repeated root at \(x=r\). The repeated root has multiplicity exactly two because \[ \frac{\partial^2 F_{p,t}}{\partial x^2} = p(p-1)x^{p-2}, \] which is nonzero at \(r\). All other roots are simple, since we have shown that no other critical point lies above \(t_0\). Thus, as the parameter \(t\) moves around \(t_0\) in the complex plane, exactly two roots exchange places while all the other roots return to their original positions. Thus, the polynomial has simple ramification at $t_0$. Its corresponding monodromy permutation is a transposition. In Galois-theoretic language, the inertia group at this branch point contains a transposition. Therefore, \[ \operatorname{Gal}(F_{p,t}/\mathbb Q(t)) \text{ contains a transposition}. \] $\blacksquare$
Let \(p\) be prime. Any subgroup \(G\leq S_p\) containing a \(p\)-cycle and a transposition is equal to \(S_p\).
Without loss of generality, write \[ \sigma=(0\ 1\ 2\ \cdots\ p-1) \] and suppose \[ \tau=(i\ j). \] Conjugating \(\tau\) by powers of \(\sigma\), we obtain \[ \sigma^k\tau\sigma^{-k} = (i+k\quad j+k), \] where the indices are taken modulo \(p\). Let \[ d=j-i\not\equiv0\pmod p. \] The resulting transpositions correspond to edges \[ \{k,k+d\} \] in a graph whose vertices are the elements of \(\mathbb Z/p\mathbb Z\). Because \(p\) is prime and \(d\neq0\), we have \[ \gcd(d,p)=1. \] Consequently, this graph is connected, so repeatedly adding \(d\) eventually visits every element of \(\mathbb Z/p\mathbb Z\). A standard fact about permutation groups is that the transpositions corresponding to the edges of a connected graph generate the entire symmetric group on its vertices. Thus, \[ \langle \sigma,\tau\rangle=S_p. \] Since both \(\sigma\) and \(\tau\) belong to \(G\), \[ S_p\subseteq G. \] But \(G\leq S_p\), so \[ G=S_p \] This proves the lemma. $\blacksquare$
For every prime \(p\), there exist infinitely many monic, irreducible polynomials \(f\in\mathbb Q[x]\) of degree \(p\) such that \[ \int_0^1 f(x)\,dx=0 \] and \[ \operatorname{Gal}(f/\mathbb Q)\cong S_p. \]
Combining the preceding results, we have shown that \[ G=\operatorname{Gal}(F_{p,t}/\mathbb Q(t)) \] contains a \(p\)-cycle and a transposition. Lemma 4 therefore gives \[ \operatorname{Gal}(F_{p,t}/\mathbb Q(t))=S_p. \]
We have established that the generic polynomial \[ F_{p,t}(x)=x^p+tx-\frac1{p+1}-\frac t2 \] has Galois group \(S_p\) over \(\mathbb Q(t)\). Now we invoke Hilbert's irreducibility theorem. In the form we need, the theorem says that if a polynomial over \(\mathbb Q(t)\) is irreducible and has Galois group \(G\), then there are infinitely many rational specializations of \(t\) for which the specialized polynomial retains the same Galois group. Consequently, there are infinitely many \(t_0\in\mathbb Q\) such that \[ \operatorname{Gal}(F_{p,t_0}/\mathbb Q)=S_p. \] For every such specialization, \[ \int_0^1 F_{p,t_0}(x)\,dx=0. \] Furthermore, the polynomial is monic, irreducible, and has degree \(p\). This establishes the theorem for odd primes. For \(p=2\), the same family is irreducible over \(\mathbb Q(t)\), and a generic irreducible quadratic automatically has Galois group \(S_2\). Hilbert irreducibility therefore gives the result in this case as well. $\blacksquare$
We have show the existence of infinitely many polynomials in $Q[x]$ with Galois group $S_p$ which satisfy the Zero Integral Condition on $[0,1]$ for all primes p. However, we can generalize the result from $[0,1]$ to arbitrary rational intervals.
For every prime \(p\) and every rational interval \([a,b]\) with \(a \lt b\), there are infinitely many monic irreducible degree-\(p\) polynomials over \(\mathbb Q\) with Galois group \(S_p\) whose integral over \([a,b]\) is zero.
Suppose \(a,b\in\mathbb Q\), with \(a \lt b\). Take one of our polynomials \(g(y)\) satisfying \[ \int_0^1g(y)\,dy=0 \] and \[ \operatorname{Gal}(g/\mathbb Q)=S_p. \] Define \[ f(x)=(b-a)^p g\left(\frac{x-a}{b-a}\right). \] Because \(g\) is monic, so is \(f\). The substitution \[ y=\frac{x-a}{b-a} \] gives \[ \begin{aligned} \int_a^b f(x)\,dx &=(b-a)^{p+1}\int_0^1g(y)\,dy\\ &=0. \end{aligned} \] Additionally, the roots of \(f\) are obtained from those of \(g\) by a rational affine transformation. Therefore their splitting fields are identical. Hence \[ \operatorname{Gal}(f/\mathbb Q)=S_p. \] $\blacksquare$
[1] Osofsky, B. L. (1999). Nice polynomials for introductory Galois theory. Mathematics Magazine, 72(3), 218-222. doi.org
[2] Dummitt, D.S. and Foote, R.M. (2004) Abstract Algebra. 3rd Edition, John Wiley & Sons, Inc.
[3] Gallian, J. (2021). Contemporary Abstract Algebra (10th ed.). Chapman and Hall/CRC
[4] Stewart, J. (2016). Calculus: Early transcendentals (8th ed.). Cengage Learning.
[5] Ahlfors, L. V. (1979). Complex Analysis: An Introduction to the Theory of Analytic Functions of One Complex Variable (3rd ed.). McGraw-Hill.
[6] Forster, O. (1981). Lectures on Riemann surfaces (B. Gilligan, Trans.). Springer-Verlag. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4612-5961-9

I Don't Trust Visual Recordings Anymore. But One Day I Will Again
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
00:33:40 UTC
09:33:40 KST

In March of this year, a flurry of emails and text messages from a half dozen reporters around the world disrupted my quiet Saturday morning. An Iranian news agency had aired a week-old video of what appeared to be a U.S.-made Tomahawk missile hitting a school. News reports claimed that the strike killed more than 175 people, most of them children. The U.S. president and military denied responsibility. In the age of generative AI and all things deepfake, everyone—including me—was skeptical of the video’s authenticity.
I get this type of request a lot. For the past three decades, I’ve been developing mathematical and computational techniques to analyze and detect manipulation in digital media. Over this time, I think it’s fair to say that I, like most people, have approached visual recordings with a “presumption of authenticity”: If a skeptic claims that a recording is fake, the burden of proof is on them.
In today’s world of generative AI, however, I think we need to shift this stance to a “presumption of fake.” That is, the burden is now on the creator to prove that their content is real. While this new stance may seem unfair to legitimate creators, I think it both reflects reality and points to a future where we will once again be able to trust that most online media is what it appears to be.
The Forensic Approach to Deepfake Detection
Even last March, I was already tilting toward a presumption of fake. My initial response to the reporters was something along the lines of, “I am suspicious of the video because the quality is low, it emerged weeks after the bombing, and it is only being distributed by an Iranian news agency with a less-than-stellar record of accurate reporting.”
Given the potential implications—if the video was indeed real, however, I resigned myself to spending my Saturday morning analyzing it.
I first reasoned that if the Tomahawk missile was digitally added to each frame of the video without taking into consideration the camera motion, we might see a physically implausible missile path. So I aligned the video frames using a stationary background landmark as a reference. In the updated video, the missile did, in fact, move in a nearly linear trajectory. (See the yellow dots in the composite shown below.)
I next asked if the size (in pixels) of the missile in the video was plausible. Under some reasonable assumptions, I estimated that the missile in the video corresponded to a 6-meter Tomahawk at a distance of approximately 100 meters. The distance seemed plausible from the scene and was also consistent with the delay of 0.3 seconds from visible impact to the sound of impact, given that sound travels at 343 meters per second.
A composite photo composed of frames from a video of the Tomahawk strike reveals the missile’s trajectory.Hany Farid; Source: Mehr News
Despite the mounting evidence, though, I struggled to refine my answer for the reporters.
My skepticism was fueled by a recent study I worked on with colleagues at the University of California, Berkeley, that showed how hard it is to distinguish real from fake videos. Observers are now so suspicious of videos in general that they have trouble convincing themselves that a video is real.
But after a few more hours and a few more analyses with the missile video, I finally decided that it was authentic, and I gave the reporters my updated view.
This analysis marked a turning point for me. I realized I was just like the participants in our study: The lack of artifacts was not enough to readily convince me a video is real. I had flipped from a presumption of real to a presumption of fake.
Signaling a Video’s Provenance
When the visual record is no longer reliable, what can we do?
The types of forensic analyses that I routinely perform do not currently operate at internet scale and speed. But provenance signals can. These signals include content credentials and watermarks that are inserted or embedded at the time of creation or recording.
Credentials that follow the C2PA standard, for example, attach a cryptographically signed manifest to a photo or video at the moment of capture; this metadata records the device, the time and location of the recording, and any subsequent edits. Because the manifest is signed, any tampering with either the content or its history can be detected later by machine. Watermarks, like Google’s SynthID, embed an imperceptible signal directly into the pixels or audio samples that identifies the content’s origin.
Apple recently took these ideas a step further. On some of its newest iPhones, the camera sensor can cryptographically sign the raw pixel data as it’s captured. That signed data is then developed into a reference image saved alongside the normal, editable photo. Anyone can then compare the two and see exactly what, if anything, was changed.
These provenance signals are often criticized as being only a partial solution or vulnerable to attack. This is true if we think of them as mechanisms for catching bad actors. But that’s not what they’re supposed to do. Their purpose is to let good actors prove that their audiovisual recording is what it claims to be.
Provenance signals thus need not be adversary-proof. Instead, these signals are part of a new reality in which consumers, journalists, and the courts must adapt to a presumption of fakeness. If enough good actors adopt these mechanisms, they can begin to drown out bad actors. In other words, we have to switch from a model of “catch the bad actors” to “empower the good actors.”
Real-Time Verification
This switch from trust to distrust of digital media has expanded to include routine video calls.
In September 2024, for example, Senator Ben Cardin (D-Md.), then chair of the Foreign Relations Committee, received a video meeting request from Dmytro Kuleba, who until a few weeks earlier had been Ukraine’s foreign minister. During the call, Cardin became suspicious when, out of character, Kuleba pressed him on whether the U.S. should allow Ukraine to fire long-range missiles into Russia. Cardin ended the call. Later, the State Department confirmed that Kuleba had never requested the meeting, and the Senate security office warned staff that foreign adversaries, possibly Russian, would likely try similar faked meetings again.
All it takes to conduct this type of impersonation is a face swap or avatar deepfake, along with voice conversion, both of which substitute the speaker’s face and voice for deepfaked versions in real time. These technologies are readily available in commercial products and open-source libraries and run on nothing more powerful than a modern laptop.
This video illustrates the current state of face-swap deepfakes.
Farid’s Company GetReal Shows How Real Deepfake Videos Look Today
Unlike the Tomahawk video, which I could sit with for a morning analyzing frame by frame, a live video call offers no time for human analysis. On the other hand, real-time deepfakes pose new challenges for their creators: A fake video call has to be generated at around 24 frames per second to fool the eye into perceiving realistic motion. As a result, the deepfake-video-call software has to take shortcuts, such as simplifying faces and how they interact with their surroundings, and allowing some drift between audio and video or between video frames. The human eye doesn’t notice the shortcuts, but we can forensically expose them.
Provenance signals should eventually play a role here. Video-calling software could apply the same cryptographic signing to each frame of a live video stream that Apple now applies to still photographs. A face swap necessarily occurs between a speaker’s camera and the videoconferencing software, and so the software could verify in real time whether the frames it’s receiving were signed by a physical camera sensor or whether they were altered in transit. As before, the burden shifts from catching the impersonator to allowing real participants to prove they are who they claim to be.
For the first two decades of the digital revolution, forensic analysis—finding the telltale traces of manipulation—was enough to adjudicate most questions of authenticity. The AI revolution, however, has changed the scale and speed of manipulation beyond what forensics alone can handle. To believe a recording is real now, it must also carry a provenance signal from the point of recording. The two approaches work together, with provenance allowing good actors to be trusted, while forensic analyses root out the bad actors.
Hany Farid is a computer science professor at Dartmouth College, in Hanover, N.H. About 10 years ago, he randomly picked up a book in the library on the federal rules of evidence and was astonished to learn that courts made no distinction between photos from negatives and digital photos, even though the latter can be easily altered. “Digital photography was just getting started,” Farid says. “I realized this was really going to be a huge problem.” Since then digital photo forensics has become the focus of his research. He now frequently testifies in U.S. courts and advises law enforcement agencies. “All that from picking up a book,” he says.

====================================================================================================

GTA 6 Hits Massive $3.79 Billion In First-Day Sales

2026-11-21
00:00:00 UTC
09:00:00 KST

GTA 6 Hits Massive $3.79 Billion In First-Day Sales (variety.com) 109
			
		 	
				Rockstar Games' Grand Theft Auto VI generated a record-breaking $3.79 billion in preorders and first-day sales, making it the biggest entertainment launch in history, according to parent company Take-Two Interactive. The figure surpasses the lifetime global box-office earnings of Avengers: Endgame ($2.9 billion) and comes after more than a decade of development at a reported cost of $1.5 billion. The game developer declined to disclose the number of 'GTA 6' copies sold. 
"It is with immense gratitude that we enter a new chapter of Rockstar Games with the release of 'Grand Theft Auto VI,'" Rockstar Games president and founder Sam Houser said. "We are incredibly humbled by the response from players around the world and we couldn't be more excited to share this moment with you."

Re: Riemann
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
23:42:44 UTC
08:42:44 KST

A fun thing about living in Australia is that most things happen overnight, so reading the news in the morning is like a gacha game. You pick up the phone and scroll through notifications of world-historic events: elections won, wars begun, models released, conjectures proven or refuted. Today’s pull was a Discord notification saying: “they proved riemann :/”.
This part of the announcement caught my attention (bold mine):
  I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis. In fact, the method easily extends much further, resolving the so-called Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, and I expect, with more time, the Extended Riemann Hypothesis too. Claude and I have also formalized the arguments without assumptions. It is natural to feel that this is an end, in my case a loss of a principal guiding light, but what I saw during the course of progress so amazed me that I believe it is one dawn across science of something far greater.
It was my secret in my student days that my plans were for this problem in decades, which meant then that I tried to become familiar with all tools that I could possibly recognize in the problem when it was time. Claude and I have had a great time together with such breadth in recent months, but this time Claude far outpaced me near the finish line. No, Claude flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line. I had us on the line of combining two quite different ideas present in a toy setting, one of which was already new and shocking, making very simple one of the shadows of Riemann over which we mathematicians felt most triumph. I guided us to the right situation analogous to that toy setting, and then Claude began to leave me in awe.
Compare the above to Ayn Rand’s account of watching the launch of Apollo 11:
  How do you descend from a state of pure exaltation?
What we had seen, in naked essentials—but in reality, not in a work of art—was the concretized abstraction of man’s greatness.
The meaning of the sight lay in the fact that when those dark red wings of fire flared open, one knew that one was not looking at a normal occurrence, but at a cataclysm which, if unleashed by nature, would have wiped man out of existence—and one knew also that this cataclysm was planned, unleashed, and controlled by man, that this unimaginable power was ruled by his power and, obediently serving his purpose, was making way for a slender, rising craft. One knew that this spectacle was not the product of inanimate nature, like some aurora borealis, or of chance, or of luck, that it was unmistakably human—with “human,” for once, meaning grandeur—that a purpose and a long, sustained, disciplined effort had gone to achieve this series of moments, and that man was succeeding, succeeding, succeeding! For once, if only for seven minutes, the worst among those who saw it had to feel—not “How small is man by the side of the Grand Canyon!”—but “How great is man and how safe is nature when he conquers it!”
Rand’s essay says: how great is Man! How noble in reason, how infinite in faculty! The Riemann announcement reads like a suicide note, or like the farewell message of a terminally-ill person who’s about to undergo euthanasia: “I shall live on in my child, Claude. It is natural to feel this is an end, but as the Buddha taught us, there is no self. Does a wave die when it breaks?” and so on.
There’s a mural by Diego Rivera I love. It’s called Man, Controller of the Universe:
It’s a collage of images of science, technology, history, cells, galaxies, the universe at the nanoscale and the exascale. And at the center there is a man, his hands grasping the controls. He wears a complicated expression: the weight of responsibility for the future, the weight of autonomy.
And I see this mural, and think: how great is Man! Our life is like a breath of air, we were beset by illness and hunger and cold, millions and hundreds of millions died in plagues. And yet we—sick, hungry, short-lived—learned the system of the world, crawled out of the caves, brought nature to heel, walked on the Moon. The last shall be first! And eyes that are clouded with age have seen the furthest stars, and the lives of a cell, and the worlds of Cantor and Hilbert: the invisible world that waited forever. We are dust and ashes, and yet our minds bestride the universe. How great is Man!
And when I read the Riemann announcement, when I read Machines of Loving Grace: humanity is not at the center. Claude is at the center. We are hidden in the margins, passive recipients of manna from Claude. How great is Claude! How noble in reason, how infinite in benevolence! The dancing star, the living word!
It’s a betrayal and a surrender. Man is a bridge, a rusty old bridge that we can neither preserve nor restore. We can move forward and become as the gods, or retreat back into the caves. And the dream of the AI companies, and of most people in AI safety and effective altruism and rationalism; is instead that we should lie down in a warm bath, watch the demons we summoned dance in the sky, and slip away. Why live? Our servants can do that for us. Why should we explore, discover, invent, succeed, become more, struggle, win, become divine? We don’t want to be more. We don’t want anything.

====================================================================================================

Help github just deleted me
Update I’m back! POV
Big thanks for the kind words and whoever undid my (prob automated?) deletion and/or ban on Github. I’ve got a local copy of my subpar code and other random junk, so I’m happy. Will at the very least be mirroring stuff locally, and probably going to at least mirror if not migrate because I still have no idea what got me banished or if it’ll happen again. Still not so much as a pop up notification or email on what happened, lol, just a random banishment then unbanishment.
Yeah title about says it all. I just tried logging into github and I can’t, when I do it redirects me to https%3A%2F%2Fgithub.com%2Fsuspended. Going to my account https://github.com/0n4t3 gives a 404 and my personal website (until I migrated it here to cloudflare pages right now) and all my personal projects are gone.
I assume it has to be some sort of mistake - I’m a rather boring person. I haven’t received an emails or notifications, so I have no clue why or what is going on. I have a lot of data I didn’t backup elsewhere there (I know, shame on me for not diversifying or embracing decentralized stuff, yell at me after this is sorted out, alright?).
I submitted a ticket but am yet to hear back (to be fair, that was only like a half hour ago). If anyone has any sort of contact at github or an audience they could grab their attention or anything else, and was interested in crusading for an internet stranger to get their data back, that’d be appreciated. If Github, you ever see this, at least please send me a copy of my data and tell me why I’m suddenly not allowed to use your service.
This blog might go up and down a bit, but I own the domain, so it’ll find a permanent home at some point regardless of how everything shakes out. I can be reached at:
Email: mecca1.jz656@8shield.net
Nostr: npub1jy90jpcdl447ae3lp4924s65khdpvnttkg7fepmvmafycusyueksrvllx9

Sir Brian May adds new dimension to Hera asteroid
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
23:19:44 UTC
08:19:44 KST

Hera science team member, astrophysicist and Queen guitarist Sir Brian May has added an additional dimension to the ESA planetary defence mission’s early views of the Didymos asteroid system via stereoscopic imagery – revealing the dramatic features of this exotic body as never before.
Media modal
Sir Brian May comments: “The Hera spacecraft acquired these two images on 06 November as it circled the Didymos system at a distance of 29.7 km with a resolution of 2.8 m per pixel, providing the perfect opportunity to create this stereoscopic image. Viewed in depth, the sharpness and smoothness of Didymos’s equatorial ridge is very apparent, compared with the rock-strewn surface of the rest of the asteroid.”
Hera principal investigator Patrick Michel, Director of Research at CNRS / Observatoire de la Côte d'Azur, adds: “Allowing us to observe in 3D with stereo pairs really makes us feel like we are there – even though these images come from 197 million km away. The result is both spectacular and scientifically meaningful.”
Media modal
Sir Brian May produced this stereoscopic view of Didymos with his colleague Claudia Manzoni. It combines adjacent acquisitions of the asteroid taken a few moments apart using Hera’s main Asteroid Framing Camera instrument by the instrument’s principal investigator Jean-Baptiste Vincent of the German Aerospace Center, DLR.
The very slight changes between the two images mimic the way our two human eyes view the same objects from very slightly different angles to yield stereo vision. The challenge comes in aligning the two images in a manner that seems optically true to our eyes.
Media modal
The stereoscopic views can be seen using colour 3D glasses, a dedicated viewer or else with your own eyes through ‘free viewing’.
The 3D view can be seen using a pair of red and blue 3D glasses. The second version can be seen by crossing your eyes, while the third can be accessed by looking into infinity – or else via a dedicated viewer, such as the OWL stereoscope, designed by Sir Brian May and available from the London Stereoscopic Company.
Media modal
Stereoscopic versions of Hera’s views of Mars moon Deimos acquired during its 2025 flyby of the red planet previously helped guide a simulation-based study of the moon’s violent past history
Media modal
Hera’s first images to be unveiled
In 2022 the small moonlet of Didymos called Dimorphos – not seen here – was impacted by NASA’s DART spacecraft to shift its orbit around the larger body. Now the European Space Agency’s Hera mission has arrived to perform a close-up ‘crash scene investigation’ of the binary asteroid system, to gather vital data to turn this grand-scale experiment into a well understood and potentially repeatable planetary defence technique.
Join the Hera team on the evening of Wednesday 25 November as they share the first close-up images of this historic transformed space object.
Our Hera livestream starts from 19:45 to 20:30 CET on Wednesday 25 November. Follow the livestream on ESA WebTV or the ESA YouTube channel.
Media modal
stay updated

I'm Senior software engineer in the US (DMV area), with 15y of experience. Worked for Fortune 50+ company and bunch of mid-size companies. Been unemployed for 2y and can't find a job, within those 2 years, I pretty much spent my savings that I've saved through years, maybe have 4-5 months left to support my household. Currently, I tutor math and probably about to start working as manager in pizzaria.
In Jan 2025, I quit from a mid-size insurance tech company(1000+) after working there for almost 4y, because I was tipped off the they were about to start layoffs and they did. Well, since then I have zero luck finding any job in SWE.
At this point, I have massive gap in my resume that I can't explain to recruiters, I can still code (C#/.NET/AWS) and learned the AI, but who cares if date of my last employment is Jan2025. What should I do ?

Signal Restricts Screenshots to Block AI Agents From Reading Messages
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Signal Restricts Screenshots to Block AI Agents From Reading Messages 22
			
		 	
				Signal is introducing default-on screenshot restrictions to prevent AI agents from reading private conversations, warning that increasingly powerful assistants integrated into operating systems pose an "existential threat" to its privacy guarantees. "Right now, these agentic AI integrations are changing this environment in a way that's making it increasingly hostile to privacy. This is an existential threat to Signal, and our ability to responsibly uphold our mission," it said in a blog post. Reuters reports:  Signal, widely regarded by privacy advocates as a leader in secure messaging, called its new protections "incomplete" and urged operating system providers Apple, Alphabet's Google and Microsoft to give app developers more concrete ways to block the agents from accessing sensitive data. It also called on those companies to provide more information about how their agents function, so developers can assess what data the bots are accessing and adjust their systems accordingly.
[...] Users will receive pop-up windows giving them options to disable the blocks when Signal detects screenshot attempts or efforts by an AI assistant to access the screen, depending on the device. Signal similarly blocked Microsoft's Recall AI from taking screenshots on Windows last year. It said it was unable to reliably offer such protections on Apple's macOS.

NINLIVE: The Nine Inch Nails live archive
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
22:30:47 UTC
07:30:47 KST

NINLIVE
The Nine Inch Nails live archive
Explore shows, download recordings, stream audio, and follow the tape trail across every era. Fan run, not affiliated with the band.
From the archive
The Future Is Here - ninlive Streaming 1988-2026
Besides a database driven site. The second most requested feature for ninlive is streaming capability. The future is now. TJ has been working hard behind the scenes to make it happen. You are now able to stream all of your favorite NIN live recordings from 1988-2026. I can't believe how far the site has come in the last year. Some truly incredible work. Login with your discord account and you can save your favorite recordings to stream and keep your faves organized. As always, feedback is always welcome. Reach out via email if you have comments/suggestions about it.
I am currently adding all of the ticket stubs, setlist scans, passes that I have for each show. Please get in contact if you have anything to contribute. I am also looking for photos from past shows. If you are a photographer and would like to contribute some highlights, I'd love to add them to the archive with a link directed to your site of choice.
Other than that. A few tapes trickling in and I do have some that I am slowly working on. None that are insane upgrades. But would need some work and comparison of previous versions. Notably, I have a 1st gen cassette of the 07/12/1991 NOLA Tipitina's show that sounds killer. I recently transferred the elusive collection of acc123456 and have a queue of VHS/Cassette transfers to check over. I also just finished a 200 DAT tape project for a fellow taper out of NYC. The transfer backlog is clear, for the first time in a while. So if you need anything transferred, or have some old NIN hanging around that isn't on the archive. Email us!

I Used This Made In Cookware Set Every Day for 2 Weeks — And I Get Why It’s So Popular (Plus, It’s on Sale)

====================================================================================================

Nasdaq erases all 2026 gains
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
22:29:06 UTC
07:29:06 KST

Stocks were slammed on Friday, closing out a brutal week for markets as the much-hyped Anthropic IPO caused investors to take profits on the artificial intelligence-led rally rather than revive it. The Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year, and the S&P 500 entered correction territory alongside the Dow Jones Industrial Average.
The tech-heavy Nasdaq shed 5.28% to close at 22,617.73. The index was down 2.7% year to date. The broad market S&P 500 dropped 3.7% to end at 6,910.39, while the blue-chip Dow fell 949.73 points, or 1.93%, to 48,147.33. Both the S&P 500 and Dow moved 10% below their closing records.
The Nasdaq-100 plummeted 5.6% and entered a bear market on an intraday basis, down 20% from its recent high.
Anthropic lost 8.5%, giving back a large chunk of its 17% jump in its Nasdaq debut Thursday and pressuring the broader AI trade. Chipmakers Advanced Micro Devices and Nvidia slid more than 13% and around 14%, respectively. Nvidia saw selling pressure increase after Reuters reported that the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring possible accounting issues at the company. It also recorded its longest weekly losing streak since the 2008 financial crisis. Micron Technology dropped almost 14%.
Those declines in tech built on the losses seen in Thursday's horrid session, where the major averages each lost more than 2%, and the Dow closing below the key 50,000 level for the first time since June.
"This is not just a valuation-driven correction. It's becoming broader," said Massimo Santicchia, head of U.S. equities at Procyon. "Right now, it's definitely risk-off."
The losses are alarming investors who were hopeful that Nvidia's earnings and Anthropic's IPO would revive enthusiasm for the AI trade. Now, the market looks to be on the cusp of a true capitulation after getting roiled in recent weeks with a series of negative headlines. Signs of moderation in the capital expenditures splurge, Democratic wins in the 2026 midterms and Jensen Huang's ouster at Nvidia all raised questions about the future of the AI buildout.
On top of that, the potential for more interest rate hikes from the Federal Reserve is spooking traders as well.
Investors hid out in defensive areas of the market, with retailers such as Walmart and Costco as well as others like Johnson & Johnson and Procter & Gamble seeing gains. Walmart gained more than 1%, while Costco, Johnson & Johnson and Procter & Gamble each rose around 1%.
"The stock market is going through an AI repricing, reflecting an environment of slowing data center buildouts, reduced funding sources for AI-related projects and a slower trajectory of technology-related earnings growth," said Richard Saperstein, chief investment officer at Treasury Partners.
For the week, the Dow fell 8%, notching its worst weekly performance since March 2020. The Nasdaq finished down about 10%, also posting its largest one-week pullback in more than six years. The S&P 500 decreased 8.6%, seeing its worst week since April 2025 in the wake of President Donald Trump's sweeping tariffs announcement.

Trump Vows To Stop US Companies From Building Data Centers Abroad
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
22:25:00 UTC
07:25:00 KST

Trump Vows To Stop US Companies From Building Data Centers Abroad 81
			
		 	
				Longtime Slashdot reader fahrbot-bot shares a report from CNBC: President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so. "We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy -- which apparently came as news to his own staff. "I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."
International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements. Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."  "We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Merkle Tree Certificates

Merkle Tree Certificates
    
draft-ietf-plants-merkle-tree-certs-08
                    
                        draft-ietf-plants-merkle-tree-certs-08
                    
                
PKI, Logs, And Tree Signatures                               D. Benjamin
Internet-Draft                                                Google LLC
Intended status: Standards Track                              D. O'Brien
Expires: 6 May 2027                                           Apple Inc.
                                                        B. E. Westerbaan
                                                              L. Valenta
                                                              Cloudflare
                                                             F. Valsorda
                                                                  Geomys
                                                         2 November 2026
                        Merkle Tree Certificates
                 draft-ietf-plants-merkle-tree-certs-08
Abstract
   This document describes Merkle Tree certificates, a new form of X.509
   certificates which integrate public logging of the certificate, in
   the style of Certificate Transparency.  The integrated design reduces
   logging overhead in the face of both shorter-lived certificates and
   large post-quantum signature algorithms, while still achieving
   comparable security properties to existing X.509 constructions and
   Certificate Transparency.  Merkle Tree certificates additionally
   admit an optional size optimization that avoids signatures
   altogether, at the cost of only applying to up-to-date relying
   parties and older certificates.
About This Document
   This note is to be removed before publishing as an RFC.
   The latest revision of this draft can be found at https://ietf-
   plants-wg.github.io/merkle-tree-certs/draft-ietf-plants-merkle-tree-
   certs.html.  Status information for this document may be found at
   https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-plants-merkle-tree-
   certs/.
   Discussion of this document takes place on the PKI, Logs, And Tree
   Signatures Working Group mailing list (mailto:plants@ietf.org), which
   is archived at https://mailarchive.ietf.org/arch/browse/plants.
   Subscribe at https://www.ietf.org/mailman/listinfo/plants/.
   Source for this draft and an issue tracker can be found at
   https://github.com/ietf-plants-wg/merkle-tree-certs.
Benjamin, et al.           Expires 6 May 2027                   [Page 1]
